❯ train.csv

  building_id - Foreign key for the building metadata.
  meter - The meter id code. Read as {0: electricity, 1: chilledwater, 2:
  steam, 3: hotwater}. Not every building has all meter types.
  timestamp - When the measurement was taken
  meter_reading - The target variable. Energy consumption in kWh (or
  equivalent). Note that this is real data with measurement error, which we
  expect will impose a baseline level of modeling error. UPDATE: as discussed
  here, the site 0 electric meter readings are in kBTU.
  building_meta.csv

  site_id - Foreign key for the weather files.
  building_id - Foreign key for training.csv
  primary_use - Indicator of the primary category of activities for the
  building based on EnergyStar property type definitions
  square_feet - Gross floor area of the building
  year_built - Year building was opened
  floor_count - Number of floors of the building
  weather_[train/test].csv

  Weather data from a meteorological station as close as possible to the site.

  site_id
  air_temperature - Degrees Celsius
  cloud_coverage - Portion of the sky covered in clouds, in oktas
  dew_temperature - Degrees Celsius
  precip_depth_1_hr - Millimeters
  sea_level_pressure - Millibar/hectopascals
  wind_direction - Compass direction (0-360)
  wind_speed - Meters per second
  test.csv

  The submission files use row numbers for ID codes in order to save space on
  the file uploads. test.csv has no feature data; it exists so you can get your
  predictions into the correct order.

  row_id - Row id for your submission file
  building_id - Building id code
  meter - The meter id code
  timestamp - Timestamps for the test data period
  sample_submission.csv

  A valid sample submission.

  All floats in the solution file were truncated to four decimal places; we
  recommend you do the same to save space on your file upload.
  There are gaps in some of the meter readings for both the train and test
  sets. Gaps in the test set are not revealed or scored.
  Based on this description write the description of the data, add
  reccomendations for aggregation

         1 ## Data description
         2
         3 **`train.csv`** — the target table, one row per `building_id`
           × `meter` × hourly `timestamp` (2016).
         4 - `building_id` — FK to `building_metadata.csv`.
         5 - `meter` — meter type code: `{0: electricity, 1: chilledwater
           , 2: steam, 3: hotwater}`. Not every building reports every me
           ter type, so the table is a ragged panel, not a full building
           × meter × hour cross-product.
         6 - `timestamp` — hourly reading time.
         7 - `meter_reading` — **target**, energy consumption in kWh (kBT
           U for site 0 electricity, per the competition's known unit cor
           rection). Real-world metering data: expect noise, dropouts, an
           d a physical floor at zero, not a clean synthetic signal.
         8
         9 **`building_metadata.csv`** — one row per `building_id`, the b
           uilding-level dimension table.
        10 - `site_id` — FK to the weather tables; groups buildings that
           share one weather station.
        11 - `building_id` — FK to `train.csv` / `test.csv`.
        12 - `primary_use` — EnergyStar activity category (categorical, l
           ow cardinality).
        13 - `square_feet` — gross floor area.
        14 - `year_built`, `floor_count` — building age and height; both
           are known to be sparsely populated.
        15
        16 **`weather_train.csv` / `weather_test.csv`** — one row per `si
           te_id` × hourly `timestamp`, from the nearest meteorological s
           tation (not the building itself).
        17 - `site_id` — FK to `building_metadata.csv`.
        18 - `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature` — °C.
        19 - `cloud_coverage` — oktas (0–8, discrete).
        20 - `precip_depth_1_hr` — mm.
        21 - `sea_level_pressure` — mb/hPa.
        22 - `wind_direction` — compass degrees (0–360, circular — not li
           nearly ordered).
        23 - `wind_speed` — m/s.
        24
        25 **`test.csv`** — same key columns as `train.csv` (`row_id`, `b
           uilding_id`, `meter`, `timestamp`) but no `meter_reading`; def
           ines the rows to predict and their submission order. Gaps in t
           he test period are not scored, so don't assume the panel is co
           mplete there either.
        26
        27 **Grain summary**
        28
        29 | File | Grain | Key |
        30 |---|---|---|
        31 | `building_metadata.csv` | one row per building | `building_i
           d` (→ `site_id`) |
        32 | `train.csv` / `test.csv` | one row per building × meter × ho
           ur | `building_id`, `meter`, `timestamp` |
        33 | `weather_[train/test].csv` | one row per site × hour | `site
           _id`, `timestamp` |
        34
        35 ## Recommendations for aggregation / joining
        36
        37 - **Join order and direction:** `train` is the base table; lef
           t-join `building_metadata` on `building_id`, then left-join we
           ather on `[site_id, timestamp]`. Always left-join *from* train
           , never inner-join — an inner join can silently drop meter-rea
           ding rows whenever a site's weather station has a gap (see the
            missing-hours check in §3), which would corrupt both training
            coverage and, if repeated on `test`, the submission's row cou
           nt.
        38 - **Don't aggregate train down to daily/site level for modelin
           g.** The target and the metric (RMSLE) are evaluated at hourly
            building×meter grain, so any grouping is for EDA/diagnostics
           only, not for producing model inputs.
        39 - **Weather gap filling happens before the join, at `(site_id,
            timestamp)` grain**, not after: interpolate short gaps (e.g.
           `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature`) linearly in time; for `c
           loud_coverage` (discrete, often sparse) prefer forward-fill or
            a per-site median rather than interpolation. Do this per `sit
           e_id` — pooling across sites will blend different climates.
        40 - **Aggregate `building_metadata` missingness by `site_id`, no
           t globally** — §4 of the EDA guide suggests missingness in `ye
           ar_built`/`floor_count` may be a site-level (data-source) arti
           fact rather than random; a per-site missing-indicator can carr
           y more signal than the raw imputed value.
        41 - **When summarizing the target for EDA (distributions, outlie
           r scan), group by `meter` first, and often by `[site_id, meter
           ]` or `[building_id, meter]` second** — the four meter types h
           ave structurally different scales and seasonality (heating vs.
            cooling load), and pooling them before grouping hides that.
        42 - **Long zero-runs are a per-series property**: compute them g
           rouped by `[building_id, meter]` (as in §6), not per row — a s
           ingle broken meter can otherwise look like many independent lo
           w readings rather than one contiguous outage.
        43 - **Any train/test comparison (§6 of the guide) must aggregate
            to the same key** (`building_id`, `meter`, or `site_id`) on b
           oth sides before comparing, since raw row-level comparison is
           meaningless across possibly different time ranges.

         1 ## Data description
         2
         3 **`train.csv`** — the target table, one row per `building_id`
           × `meter` × hourly `timestamp` (2016).
         4 - `building_id` — FK to `building_metadata.csv`.
         5 - `meter` — meter type code: `{0: electricity, 1: chilledwater
           , 2: steam, 3: hotwater}`. Not every building reports every me
           ter type, so the table is a ragged panel, not a full building
           × meter × hour cross-product.
         6 - `timestamp` — hourly reading time.
         7 - `meter_reading` — **target**, energy consumption. Real-world
            metering data: expect noise, dropouts, and a physical floor a
           t zero, not a clean synthetic signal.
         8
         9 **Meter types and units.** `meter_reading` is *not* on one con
           sistent physical scale across meter types (or even across site
           s), because each meter measures a different form of delivered
           energy and the raw data mixes unit systems:
        10
        11 | `meter` | Type | What it measures | Nominal unit | Known qui
           rks |
        12 |---|---|---|---|---|
        13 | 0 | electricity | electrical energy consumed | kWh | **Site
           0 is actually in kBTU, not kWh** (a documented data error) — m
           ust be converted (`× 0.2931`) before pooling site 0 with other
            sites, or modeled with a per-site correction. |
        14 | 1 | chilledwater | thermal energy delivered for cooling | kW
           h (energy equivalent, often derived from flow × ΔT) | Zero whe
           n a building has no active cooling season/system — long legiti
           mate zero-runs, not just outages. |
        15 | 2 | steam | thermal energy delivered as steam (heating) | kW
           h (energy equivalent) | Sparse — mostly campus/district-steam
           buildings; heavy seasonality (winter-only demand). |
        16 | 3 | hotwater | thermal energy delivered as hot water (heatin
           g) | kWh (energy equivalent) | Similar seasonality to steam; c
           an overlap with steam use in the same building. |
        17
        18 Practical implications:
        19 - Because the four meters are different physical quantities on
            (mostly) the same nominal unit but with a site-specific unit
           bug, **never sum or average `meter_reading` across `meter` typ
           es** — treat each meter type as its own target distribution (s
           ee §5, target analysis, which already groups by meter).
        20 - Apply the site-0 electricity kBTU→kWh conversion once, early
            in preprocessing, before any cross-site or cross-meter aggreg
           ation — otherwise site-0 electricity readings will look ~3.4×
           larger than they should and will distort both EDA plots and an
           y model that pools sites.
        21 - Steam/hot-water/chilled-water zero-runs are frequently a rea
           l seasonal absence of demand rather than a broken sensor, unli
           ke electricity, which normally never reads a sustained zero fo
           r an occupied building — factor this into the outage vs. off-s
           eason judgement in §6.
        22
        23 **`building_metadata.csv`** — one row per `building_id`, the b
           uilding-level dimension table.
        24 - `site_id` — FK to the weather tables; groups buildings that
           share one weather station.
        25 - `building_id` — FK to `train.csv` / `test.csv`.
        26 - `primary_use` — EnergyStar activity category (categorical, l
           ow cardinality).
        27 - `square_feet` — gross floor area.
        28 - `year_built`, `floor_count` — building age and height; both
           are known to be sparsely populated.
        29
        30 **`weather_train.csv` / `weather_test.csv`** — one row per `si
           te_id` × hourly `timestamp`, from the nearest meteorological s
           tation (not the building itself).
        31 - `site_id` — FK to `building_metadata.csv`.
        32 - `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature` — °C.
        33 - `cloud_coverage` — oktas (0–8, discrete).
        34 - `precip_depth_1_hr` — mm.
        35 - `sea_level_pressure` — mb/hPa.
        36 - `wind_direction` — compass degrees (0–360, circular — not li
           nearly ordered).
        37 - `wind_speed` — m/s.
        38
        39 **`test.csv`** — same key columns as `train.csv` (`row_id`, `b
           uilding_id`, `meter`, `timestamp`) but no `meter_reading`; def
           ines the rows to predict and their submission order. Gaps in t
           he test period are not scored, so don't assume the panel is co
           mplete there either.
        40
        41 **Grain summary**
        42
        43 | File | Grain | Key |
        44 |---|---|---|
        45 | `building_metadata.csv` | one row per building | `building_i
           d` (→ `site_id`) |
        46 | `train.csv` / `test.csv` | one row per building × meter × ho
           ur | `building_id`, `meter`, `timestamp` |
        47 | `weather_[train/test].csv` | one row per site × hour | `site
           _id`, `timestamp` |
        48
        49 ## Recommendations for aggregation / joining
        50
        51 - **Join order and direction:** `train` is the base table; lef
           t-join `building_metadata` on `building_id`, then left-join we
           ather on `[site_id, timestamp]`. Always left-join *from* train
           , never inner-join — an inner join can silently drop meter-rea
           ding rows whenever a site's weather station has a gap (see the
            missing-hours check in §3), which would corrupt both training
            coverage and, if repeated on `test`, the submission's row cou
           nt.
        52 - **Don't aggregate train down to daily/site level for modelin
           g.** The target and the metric (RMSLE) are evaluated at hourly
            building×meter grain, so any grouping is for EDA/diagnostics
           only, not for producing model inputs.
        53 - **Weather gap filling happens before the join, at `(site_id,
            timestamp)` grain**, not after: interpolate short gaps (e.g.
           `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature`) linearly in time; for `c
           loud_coverage` (discrete, often sparse) prefer forward-fill or
            a per-site median rather than interpolation. Do this per `sit
           e_id` — pooling across sites will blend different climates.
        54 - **Aggregate `building_metadata` missingness by `site_id`, no
           t globally** — §4 of the EDA guide suggests missingness in `ye
           ar_built`/`floor_count` may be a site-level (data-source) arti
           fact rather than random; a per-site missing-indicator can carr
           y more signal than the raw imputed value.
        55 - **When summarizing the target for EDA (distributions, outlie
           r scan), group by `meter` first, and often by `[site_id, meter
           ]` or `[building_id, meter]` second** — the four meter types h
           ave structurally different scales, units (site-0 electricity),
            and seasonality (heating vs. cooling load), and pooling them
           before grouping hides that.
        56 - **Long zero-runs are a per-series property**: compute them g
           rouped by `[building_id, meter]` (as in §6), not per row — a s
           ingle broken meter can otherwise look like many independent lo
           w readings rather than one contiguous outage. For steam/chille
           dwater/hotwater, cross-check candidate zero-runs against the s
           eason (§6) before labeling them outages, since off-season zero
           s are expected.
        57 - **Any train/test comparison (§6 of the guide) must aggregate
            to the same key** (`building_id`, `meter`, or `site_id`) on b
           oth sides before comparing, since raw row-level comparison is
           meaningless across possibly different time ranges.

  data_aggregation.ipynb (Homeworks/Homework%201/data_aggregation.ipynb) with:

  - Load & unit fix — reads all three tables with downcast dtypes, converts
    site-0 electricity from kBTU→kWh.
  - Weather cleaning — per-site interpolation: linear for
    temperature/pressure/wind speed, circular (sin/cos) for wind direction,
    forward/back-fill for cloud coverage, zero-fill for precipitation.
  - Merge — left-join train/test ← building_metadata ← weather, with a row-count
    assertion to catch accidental fan-out.
  - Sanity checks — verifies row counts match the raw target tables and reports
    remaining missingness.
  - Save — writes train_merged.parquet / test_merged.parquet to data/processed/.
  - Recommendations section — log1p target, missing-value indicators, building
    metadata imputation strategy, time/holiday features, timezone check,
    zero-run/outage handling per meter type, dtype discipline, and
    fit-on-train-only guidance for any downstream imputation.

❯ why there are still cloud_coverage and sea_level_pressure missing

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Confirmed the mechanism. Sites 7 and 11 have 100% missing cloud_coverage for
  the whole year (that station never reported it), and site 5 has 100% missing
  sea_level_pressure.

  My clean_weather function fills gaps using only data from the same site
  (groupby("site_id") → ffill/bfill or .interpolate). That works fine when a
  site has some real readings to fill from — but when a site's column is empty
  for its entire history, there's nothing to forward-fill, back-fill, or
  interpolate from, so those rows stay NaN after cleaning. That's exactly what
  the sanity-check cell in the notebook is surfacing.

  Options to close that remaining gap (not yet applied in the notebook):
  1. Cross-site fallback — for a site missing a column entirely, borrow another
     site's series (e.g. a nearby/similar-climate site) or fall back to a global
     hourly mean across all sites, rather than leaving it NaN.
  2. Drop the column if it's this unreliable overall and rely on the
     missing-indicator + other correlated weather features instead.
  3. Leave it NaN and rely on the model (tree-based models handle NaN natively;
     for a neural net you'd still need some fill value, but at least it's tagged
     via the _was_missing indicator from §7).

  Want me to add a cross-site fallback step (e.g. per-hour global median) to the
  clean_weather function so sites 5/7/11 aren't fully NaN for these columns?

❯ Analyze interpolation table: is not that too much?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Good instinct to sanity-check this. I ran the numbers on the actual
  weather_train.csv:

  Overall % missing before any fill:

  ┌────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │       column       │ % missing │
  ├────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ cloud_coverage     │ 49.5%     │
  ├────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ precip_depth_1_hr  │ 36.0%     │
  ├────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ sea_level_pressure │ 7.6%      │
  ├────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ wind_direction     │ 4.5%      │
  ├────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ wind_speed         │ 0.2%      │
  ├────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ dew_temperature    │ 0.1%      │
  ├────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ air_temperature    │ 0.0%      │
  └────────────────────┴───────────┘

  That means my current clean_weather step in the notebook is fabricating
  roughly half of cloud_coverage (via ffill().bfill()) and over a third of
  precip_depth_1_hr (via ffill(limit=2) + zero-fill). That's too aggressive as
  written — a few problems:

  1. Not "interpolation," mostly extrapolation. ffill/bfill on a 50%-missing
     column doesn't recover a real signal, it just repeats the last known
     reading across long stretches — for sites like site 1 (80.6% missing) or
     7/11 (100% missing), that's almost entirely invented, flat, stale data, not
     the true (likely noisy, unmeasured) cloud cover.
  2. It masks a genuine "measurement doesn't exist" state as if it were
     "measured and constant." A downstream model can't tell filled-in
     cloud_coverage from a real reading unless the _was_missing indicator
     (mentioned in §7) is actually added — which the current notebook doesn't do
     yet.
  3. sea_level_pressure and wind_direction are in a different, much safer regime
     (7.6% and 4.5%) — true short-gap linear/circular interpolation there is
     reasonable and won't fabricate much.

  Recommendation: don't treat cloud_coverage and precip_depth_1_hr the same way
  as the low-missingness columns. Options, roughly in order of what I'd do:
  - cloud_coverage: given ~50% missingness with two sites at 100%, drop it from
    linear/ffill treatment and instead keep it only as a coarse feature (bucket
    into few/none/some/most-of-sky, unknown category) plus a was_missing flag —
    don't pretend to reconstruct it.
  - precip_depth_1_hr: keep the zero-fill (physically defensible — "no rain
    recorded" ≈ 0), but drop the ffill(limit=2) step, since a short
    carry-forward here isn't really justified for a quantity that changes hour
    to hour.
  - sea_level_pressure, wind_direction, wind_speed, dew_temperature,
    air_temperature: keep the current linear/circular interpolation —
    missingness is low enough (≤7.6%) that this is a reasonable, limited-gap
    fill.
  - Add the _was_missing indicator columns now (currently only recommended in §7
    text, not implemented) so the model can distinguish real vs. filled values
    everywhere this matters.

  Want me to update clean_weather in the notebook along these lines (drop
  aggressive fill for cloud_coverage/precip_depth_1_hr, add _was_missing flags)?

❯ Now consider the interpretability: does cloud coverage and precip_depth_1_hr
  are really so meaninful for understanding the wheather?

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Checked it against the actual data rather than just intuition, using the same
  Spearman-vs-log-target setup as EDA §8:

  ┌──────────┬───────┬────────┬───────┬──────┬─────────┬───────┬─────────┐
  │          │ air_t │ cloud_ │ dew_t │ prec │ sea_lev │ wind_ │ wind_sp │
  │          │  emp  │  cov   │  emp  │  ip  │  el_p   │  dir  │   eed   │
  ├──────────┼───────┼────────┼───────┼──────┼─────────┼───────┼─────────┤
  │ chilledw │ 0.431 │ 0.002  │ 0.437 │ 0.04 │ -0.092  │ -0.08 │ -0.095  │
  │ ater     │       │        │       │ 1    │         │ 0     │         │
  ├──────────┼───────┼────────┼───────┼──────┼─────────┼───────┼─────────┤
  │ electric │ 0.026 │ -0.039 │ -0.01 │ 0.00 │ -0.039  │ -0.02 │ -0.059  │
  │ ity      │       │        │ 2     │ 1    │         │ 7     │         │
  ├──────────┼───────┼────────┼───────┼──────┼─────────┼───────┼─────────┤
  │ hotwater │ -0.42 │ -0.175 │ -0.20 │ 0.00 │ 0.200   │ 0.059 │ 0.010   │
  │          │ 0     │        │ 3     │ 8    │         │       │         │
  ├──────────┼───────┼────────┼───────┼──────┼─────────┼───────┼─────────┤
  │ steam    │ -0.46 │ 0.099  │ -0.45 │ -0.0 │ 0.071   │ 0.140 │ 0.192   │
  │          │ 0     │        │ 0     │ 57   │         │       │         │
  └──────────┴───────┴────────┴───────┴──────┴─────────┴───────┴─────────┘

  precip_depth_1_hr — essentially uncorrelated with the target for every meter
  type (|ρ| ≤ 0.06). Physically that tracks: rain is a weak driver of
  HVAC/electrical load compared to temperature, and its effect (if any) is
  already captured indirectly through temperature/humidity. Given that plus 36%
  raw missingness, I'd deprioritize it — keep the zero-fill for completeness but
  don't expect it to earn its place as a feature; it's a good candidate to drop
  rather than force through a fill pipeline.

  cloud_coverage — small but non-trivial for hotwater (-0.175) and steam
  (0.099), near-zero for chilledwater/electricity. It does carry a real physical
  signal (proxy for solar gain, which offsets heating load and matters for
  daylighting), but it's weaker than air_temperature/dew_temperature and only
  loosely redundant with them (ρ=0.12 vs air_temp) — so it's not purely a
  duplicate, but combined with ~50% missingness (two sites at 100%), the
  reconstruction cost is high relative to what it adds.

  Bottom line: air_temperature and dew_temperature are doing the real work (and
  are ~73% correlated with each other, so somewhat redundant with one another
  too — worth checking VIF/dropping one later). cloud_coverage is a
  marginal-value, high-missingness feature I'd keep only as a coarse bucketed
  feature + missing-flag rather than fully reconstructed; precip_depth_1_hr
  looks safe to drop outright for this target.

         1 <a id='summary'></a>
         2 # 10. Summary & modeling implications
         3
         4 - **Target transform:** train on `log1p(meter_reading)` with M
           SE loss, `expm1` predictions — matches RMSLE exactly (§9).
         5 - **Site-0 electricity is in kBTU, not kWh** — must be unit-co
           rrected before pooling with other sites (data description).
         6 - **Never mix `meter` types when aggregating/pooling the targe
           t** — the four meters differ in scale, unit quirks, and season
           ality; group by `meter` (and often `[site_id, meter]` / `[buil
           ding_id, meter]`) for any target summary.
         7 - **Zero-runs are meter-dependent**, not uniformly "outages":
           electricity zero-runs on an occupied building are almost alway
           s sensor/meter issues, while steam/chilledwater/hotwater zero-
           runs are frequently a legitimate off-season absence of demand
           (§6). Cross-check against season before flagging as an outage.
         8 - **Weather features — keep vs. drop vs. simplify** (data desc
           ription / §8):
         9   - `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature` — strongest predictor
           s, complete data; keep both but note they're correlated with e
           ach other (ρ≈0.73), so treat as partially redundant.
        10   - `cloud_coverage` — weak-to-modest signal for hotwater/stea
           m only; ~50% missing overall (two sites 100% missing) → don't
           fully reconstruct via fill, use a coarse bucket + missing-indi
           cator instead.
        11   - `precip_depth_1_hr` — essentially uncorrelated with the ta
           rget for every meter (|ρ| ≤ 0.06) and 36% missing → good candi
           date to drop.
        12   - `sea_level_pressure`, `wind_direction`, `wind_speed` — low
            missingness, safe to interpolate normally (linear / circular
           for wind direction).
        13 - **Building metadata:** `square_feet` needs a log transform (
           heavy right skew); `year_built`/`floor_count` missingness look
           s site-dependent (data-source artifact), so impute per `site_i
           d`/`primary_use` rather than globally, and keep a missing-indi
           cator.
        14 - **Join discipline:** always left-join *from* the meter-readi
           ng table (train/test) — building and weather both have gaps th
           at would silently drop target rows under an inner join.
        15 - **Timezone check (§7):** verify per-site local-time offset b
           efore deriving hour-of-day features — `timestamp` may not be u
           niformly aligned to local solar time across sites.
        16 - **Metric:** RMSLE under-penalizes large absolute misses on l
           arge readings and over-penalizes small absolute misses near ze
           ro — track RMSLE per meter/site and MAE on `log1p` as compleme
           ntary diagnostics (§9).
        17
        18 _See [data_aggregation.ipynb](data_aggregation.ipynb) for wher
           e these decisions are implemented (unit fix, per-site weather
           interpolation, merge)._

         1 <a id='weather'></a>
         2 # 3. Weather cleaning & interpolation
         3
         4 `cloud_coverage` and `precip_depth_1_hr` are dropped upstream
           (not even read, see §1) — EDA §8 showed both are
         5 essentially uncorrelated with the target for every meter type,
            and reconstructing 36-50% missing data for no
         6 predictive payoff isn't worth it.
         7
         8 For the remaining columns, cleaning is done **before** the joi
           n, per `site_id`, on the weather table's own hourly
         9 grid — interpolating after the merge would mix timestamps from
            different building×meter series and make the
        10 per-site time index ambiguous. Two stages:
        11
        12 **Stage 1 — per-site linear/circular time interpolation** (fix
           es ordinary short gaps):
        13 - `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature`, `sea_level_pressure`,
           `wind_speed` — linear time interpolation within each
        14   site; these vary smoothly hour to hour.
        15 - `wind_direction` — circular (0-360°); linear interpolation i
           s wrong across the 0/360 wrap (e.g. 350° -> 10° would
        16   interpolate through 180° instead of through 0°). Interpolate
           d on the unit circle (sin/cos) and converted back.
        17
        18 **Stage 2 — global (cross-site) fallback**, for `sea_level_pre
           ssure`, `wind_speed`, `wind_direction` only:
        19 Per-site interpolation can only fill a gap if that site has *s
           ome* real reading to interpolate from. It does not
        20 for `sea_level_pressure` — one site (site 5) is **100% missing
           ** for the whole year, so stage 1 leaves it entirely
        21 NaN. Justification for borrowing other sites' readings here sp
           ecifically:
        22 - Sea-level pressure and wind are driven by **synoptic-scale w
           eather systems** that span hundreds of kilometers,
        23   so at the same hour, readings from other sites are a meaning
           fully informative stand-in — unlike, say,
        24   `air_temperature`, which is far more site/microclimate-speci
           fic and isn't this sparse here anyway (~0% missing).
        25 - Concretely: for any (site, hour) still missing after stage 1
           , fill with the **mean across all other sites at
        26   that same hour**. If an hour has zero sites reporting at all
            (edge case), fall back further to a plain
        27   site-agnostic linear time interpolation over the whole table
           .
        28 - `wind_direction`'s cross-site fallback is applied on its sin
           /cos decomposition (not the raw degrees) so the
        29   circular averaging stays valid, then converted back to degre
           es at the end.
        30
        31 Any value still missing after both stages (only possible if ev
           ery single site reports NaN at the same hour) is
        32 left as NaN — encode a `*_was_missing` indicator downstream (s
           ee §7) rather than guessing a value.
  ⎿  Updated cell cell-7:
         1 def clean_weather(weather: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
         2     weather = weather.sort_values(["site_id", "timestamp"]).re
           set_index(drop=True)
         3     out = weather.copy()
         4
         5     # circular decomposition for wind_direction (avoids interp
           olating across the 0/360 wrap)
         6     rad = np.deg2rad(out["wind_direction"])
         7     out["_wd_sin"] = np.sin(rad)
         8     out["_wd_cos"] = np.cos(rad)
         9
        10     local_cols = [\"air_temperature\", \"dew_temperature\"]  #
            ~0% missing, never lacking a site to interpolate from
        11     global_fallback_cols = [\"sea_level_pressure\", \"wind_spe
           ed\", \"_wd_sin\", \"_wd_cos\"]
        12     all_cols = local_cols + global_fallback_cols
        13
        14     # --- stage 1: per-site linear time interpolation for ordi
           nary, short, site-local gaps ---
        15     def interp_linear(g):
        16         g = g.set_index(\"timestamp\")
        17         g[all_cols] = g[all_cols].interpolate(method=\"time\",
            limit_direction=\"both\")
        18         return g.reset_index()
        19
        20     out = out.groupby(\"site_id\", group_keys=False).apply(int
           erp_linear).reset_index(drop=True)
        21
        22     # --- stage 2: global (cross-site) fallback, only for colu
           mns where a whole site can be
        23     # fully missing (e.g. site 5 is 100% missing sea_level_pre
           ssure) --- see markdown above for why
        24     # this is defensible for sea_level_pressure/wind specifica
           lly.
        25     for col in global_fallback_cols:
        26         missing = out[col].isna()
        27         if missing.any():
        28             hourly_mean = out.groupby(\"timestamp\")[col].tran
           sform(\"mean\")
        29             out.loc[missing, col] = hourly_mean[missing]
        30
        31     # last resort: an hour with zero sites reporting at all fa
           lls back to a site-agnostic
        32     # time interpolation over the whole table
        33     out = out.sort_values(\"timestamp\")
        34     for col in global_fallback_cols:
        35         if out[col].isna().any():
        36             out[col] = out.set_index(\"timestamp\")[col].inter
           polate(method=\"time\", limit_direction=\"both\").to_numpy()
        37     out = out.sort_values([\"site_id\", \"timestamp\"]).reset_
           index(drop=True)
        38
        39     out[\"wind_direction\"] = (np.rad2deg(np.arctan2(out[\"_wd
           _sin\"], out[\"_wd_cos\"])) + 360) % 360
        40     out = out.drop(columns=[\"_wd_sin\", \"_wd_cos\"])
        41
        42     for col in local_cols + [\"sea_level_pressure\", \"wind_sp
           eed\", \"wind_direction\"]:
        43         out[col] = out[col].astype(\"float32\")
        44     return out
        45
        46
        47 weather_train_clean = clean_weather(weather_train)
        48 weather_test_clean = clean_weather(weather_test)
        49
        50 before = weather_train.isna().mean().mul(100).round(1)
        51 after = weather_train_clean.isna().mean().mul(100).round(1)
        52 display(pd.DataFrame({\"% missing before\": before, \"% missin
           g after\": after}))

⏺ Edit Notebook(Homeworks/Homework 1/data_aggregation.ipynb@cell-14)
  ⎿  Updated cell cell-14:
         1 <a id='recs'></a>
         2 # 7. Recommendations for further processing
         3
         4 Beyond the weather cleaning done above, before this table is m
           odel-ready:
         5
         6 **Target**
         7 - Train on `log1p(meter_reading)` with an MSE loss and `expm1`
            predictions — RMSLE (the competition metric) is
         8   exactly RMSE on `log1p(y)` (see EDA §9), so this makes the l
           oss match the metric directly.
         9 - Consider modeling each `meter` type separately (or at least
           giving the model a strong `meter` feature/embedding)
        10   — the four meters have structurally different scales and sea
           sonality, and pooling them naively will bias a
        11   single shared error term toward whichever meter has the most
            rows.
        12
        13 **Weather columns dropped/kept**
        14 - `cloud_coverage` and `precip_depth_1_hr` were dropped entire
           ly (see intro / §3) — near-zero correlation with the
        15   target across all meter types plus 36-50% missingness meant
           reconstructing them wasn't worth it. If a later
        16   iteration wants `cloud_coverage` back, prefer a coarse bucke
           ted feature + missing-indicator over full
        17   interpolation (per EDA §8), rather than the aggressive fill
           this notebook used to have.
        18 - `sea_level_pressure`/`wind_speed`/`wind_direction` used a tw
           o-stage fill (per-site interpolation, then a
        19   cross-site fallback for sites missing the column entirely, e
           .g. site 5's 100%-missing `sea_level_pressure`) —
        20   add a `<col>_was_globally_filled` indicator if you want the
           model to distinguish site-local vs. borrowed values.
        21
        22 **Missing-value indicators**
        23 - Add a binary `<col>_was_missing` flag for `year_built` / `fl
           oor_count`, and for any weather column that still
        24   had missingness even after the two-stage fill. Missingness c
           an itself be informative (e.g. no chilled-water
        25   meter because the building has no active cooling system) — i
           mputing the value alone throws that signal away.
        26
        27 **Building metadata**
        28 - Impute `year_built` with a per-`primary_use`/per-`site_id` m
           edian rather than a single global constant —
        29   building age distributions differ a lot by category and by s
           ite (EDA §4 found missingness itself is site-
        30   dependent, suggesting a data-source artifact, not randomness
           ).
        31 - `floor_count` is missing for the large majority of buildings
           ; given how sparse it is, it may be more robust to
        32   drop it in favor of the missingness indicator alone, or buck
           et it into a low-cardinality band (`1`, `2-5`,
        33   `6+`, `unknown`) instead of imputing a point estimate.
        34 - `square_feet` is heavily right-skewed — use `log1p(square_fe
           et)` as the model input (already used this way in
        35   EDA §4/§8).
        36
        37 **Time features**
        38 - Derive `hour`, `day_of_week`, `month`, and a US federal holi
           day flag from `timestamp` — EDA §6 shows clear
        39   hour-of-day and day-of-week profiles, and holidays are a com
           mon cause of unexplained low-occupancy dips that a
        40   raw hour/day-of-week feature won't capture on its own.
        41 - Consider that `timestamp` in the weather/meter files may be
           in **site-local standard time**, not UTC (EDA §7's
        42   peak-hour check is exactly this diagnostic) — if a per-site
           offset is found, correct it before deriving
        43   hour-of-day features, otherwise "hour" won't align with actu
           al daylight/occupancy patterns across sites.
        44
        45 **Outage / zero-run handling**
        46 - Build the long-zero-run flag from EDA §6 (`building_id`, `me
           ter` grain) as an explicit feature or filtering
        47   mask — decide per meter type whether a zero run reflects a b
           roken sensor (electricity) or a legitimate
        48   off-season absence of demand (steam/chilledwater/hotwater),
           per the meter-type notes in EDA.
        49
        50 **Memory / pipeline**
        51 - Keep the dtype downcasting used above (`int8`/`int16`/`float
           32`/`category`) through every later transform step —
        52   it's easy for a `merge`, `fillna`, or `groupby.transform` to
            silently upcast back to `float64`/`int64`.
        53 - Do the weather cleaning and any imputation fit **on train on
           ly**, then apply the fitted
        54   statistics (medians, fill values) to `test` — never compute
           a fill value from `test`, to avoid leaking test
        55   distribution into preprocessing.
        56 - This notebook only builds the merged feature table; validati
           on-aware target encoding, scaling, and any
        57   leakage-sensitive transforms belong in the modeling pipeline
            (Stage 2+), fit inside each CV fold, not here.

         1 <a id='weather'></a>
         2 # 3. Weather cleaning & interpolation
         3
         4 `cloud_coverage` and `precip_depth_1_hr` are dropped upstream
           (not even read, see §1) — EDA §8 showed both are
         5 essentially uncorrelated with the target for every meter type,
            and reconstructing 36-50% missing data for no
         6 predictive payoff isn't worth it.
         7
         8 For the remaining columns, cleaning is done **before** the joi
           n, per `site_id`, on the weather table's own hourly
         9 grid — interpolating after the merge would mix timestamps from
            different building×meter series and make the
        10 per-site time index ambiguous. Three stages:
        11
        12 **Stage 0 — reindex each site onto the full hourly grid.** `we
           ather_[train/test].csv` doesn't just have NaN
        13 values, it's missing entire *rows* for some (site, hour) combi
           nations (§3.2 in EDA). Interpolation can't fill a
        14 row that isn't there, so each site is first reindexed onto the
            complete hourly range, turning "row absent" into
        15 "row present with NaN" — otherwise those hours would come back
            missing again after the join, no matter how good
        16 the interpolation is.
        17
        18 **Stage 1 — per-site linear/circular time interpolation** (fix
           es ordinary short gaps):
        19 - `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature`, `sea_level_pressure`,
           `wind_speed` — linear time interpolation within each
        20   site; these vary smoothly hour to hour.
        21 - `wind_direction` — circular (0-360°); linear interpolation i
           s wrong across the 0/360 wrap (e.g. 350° -> 10° would
        22   interpolate through 180° instead of through 0°). Interpolate
           d on the unit circle (sin/cos) and converted back.
        23
        24 **Stage 2 — global (cross-site) fallback**, for `sea_level_pre
           ssure`, `wind_speed`, `wind_direction` only:
        25 Per-site interpolation can only fill a gap if that site has *s
           ome* real reading to interpolate from. It does not
        26 for `sea_level_pressure` — one site (site 5) is **100% missing
           ** for the whole year, so stage 1 leaves it entirely
        27 NaN. Justification for borrowing other sites' readings here sp
           ecifically:
        28 - Sea-level pressure and wind are driven by **synoptic-scale w
           eather systems** that span hundreds of kilometers,
        29   so at the same hour, readings from other sites are a meaning
           fully informative stand-in — unlike, say,
        30   `air_temperature`, which is far more site/microclimate-speci
           fic and isn't this sparse here anyway (~0% missing).
        31 - Concretely: for any (site, hour) still missing after stage 1
           , fill with the **mean across all other sites at
        32   that same hour**. If an hour has zero sites reporting at all
            (edge case), fall back further to a plain
        33   site-agnostic linear time interpolation over the whole table
           .
        34 - `wind_direction`'s cross-site fallback is applied on its sin
           /cos decomposition (not the raw degrees) so the
        35   circular averaging stays valid, then converted back to degre
           es at the end.
        36
        37 Any value still missing after all three stages (only possible
           if every single site reports NaN at the same hour)
        38 is left as NaN — encode a `*_was_missing` indicator downstream
            (see §7) rather than guessing a value.
  ⎿  Updated cell cell-7:
         1 def clean_weather(weather: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
         2     weather = weather.sort_values(["site_id", "timestamp"]).re
           set_index(drop=True)
         3
         4     # stage 0: reindex each site onto the full hourly grid, so
            a missing (site, hour) ROW
         5     # becomes a present row with NaN values that stage 1/2 can
            actually fill.
         6     full_index = pd.date_range(weather.timestamp.min(), weathe
           r.timestamp.max(), freq="h")
         7
         8     def reindex_site(g):
         9         site_id = g["site_id"].iloc[0]
        10         g = g.drop(columns="site_id").set_index("timestamp").r
           eindex(full_index)
        11         g.insert(0, "site_id", site_id)
        12         return g.reset_index(names="timestamp")
        13
        14     out = weather.groupby("site_id", group_keys=False).apply(r
           eindex_site).reset_index(drop=True)
        15
        16     # circular decomposition for wind_direction (avoids interp
           olating across the 0/360 wrap)
        17     rad = np.deg2rad(out["wind_direction"])
        18     out["_wd_sin"] = np.sin(rad)
        19     out["_wd_cos"] = np.cos(rad)
        20
        21     local_cols = ["air_temperature", "dew_temperature"]  # ~0%
            missing, never lacking a site to interpolate from
        22     global_fallback_cols = ["sea_level_pressure", "wind_speed"
           , "_wd_sin", "_wd_cos"]
        23     all_cols = local_cols + global_fallback_cols
        24
        25     # --- stage 1: per-site linear time interpolation for ordi
           nary, short, site-local gaps ---
        26     def interp_linear(g):
        27         g = g.set_index("timestamp")
        28         g[all_cols] = g[all_cols].interpolate(method="time", l
           imit_direction="both")
        29         return g.reset_index()
        30
        31     out = out.groupby("site_id", group_keys=False).apply(inter
           p_linear).reset_index(drop=True)
        32
        33     # --- stage 2: global (cross-site) fallback, only for colu
           mns where a whole site can be
        34     # fully missing (e.g. site 5 is 100% missing sea_level_pre
           ssure) --- see markdown above for why
        35     # this is defensible for sea_level_pressure/wind specifica
           lly.
        36     for col in global_fallback_cols:
        37         missing = out[col].isna()
        38         if missing.any():
        39             hourly_mean = out.groupby("timestamp")[col].transf
           orm("mean")
        40             out.loc[missing, col] = hourly_mean[missing]
        41
        42     # last resort: an hour with zero sites reporting at all fa
           lls back to a site-agnostic
        43     # time interpolation over the whole table
        44     out = out.sort_values("timestamp")
        45     for col in global_fallback_cols:
        46         if out[col].isna().any():
        47             out[col] = out.set_index("timestamp")[col].interpo
           late(method="time", limit_direction="both").to_numpy()
        48     out = out.sort_values(["site_id", "timestamp"]).reset_inde
           x(drop=True)
        49
        50     out["wind_direction"] = (np.rad2deg(np.arctan2(out["_wd_si
           n"], out["_wd_cos"])) + 360) % 360
        51     out = out.drop(columns=["_wd_sin", "_wd_cos"])
        52
        53     for col in local_cols + ["sea_level_pressure", "wind_speed
           ", "wind_direction"]:
        54         out[col] = out[col].astype("float32")
        55     return out
        56

         1 def clean_weather(w: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
         2     hours = pd.date_range(w.timestamp.min(), w.timestamp.max()
           , freq="h")
         3     grid = pd.MultiIndex.from_product(
         4         [w.site_id.unique(), hours], names=["site_id", "timest
           amp"]
         5     )
         6     w = w.set_index(["site_id", "timestamp"]).reindex(grid).re
           set_index()
         7
         8     rad = np.deg2rad(w.pop("wind_direction"))
         9     w["wd_sin"], w["wd_cos"] = np.sin(rad), np.cos(rad)
        10
        11     cols = w.columns.drop(["site_id", "timestamp"])
        12     w[cols] = w.groupby("site_id")[cols].transform(
        13         lambda s: s.interpolate(limit_direction="both")
        14     )
        15     w[cols] = w[cols].fillna(w.groupby("timestamp")[cols].tran
           sform("mean"))
        16
        17     w["wind_direction"] = np.rad2deg(np.arctan2(w.pop("wd_sin"
           ), w.pop("wd_cos"))) % 360
        18     return w.astype({c: "float32" for c in WEATHER_COLS})
        19

        22 weather_test_clean = clean_weather(weather_test)

           sna().mean() * 100,

❯ I wondering what to do with "meter" column: I think 0 1 2 3 will nnot be
  suitable for training ML model and Mulilayer perceptron. But this column is,
  obviously, the most definite information about target.

  Provide your reccomendations on potential actions with this feature

⏺ You're right that 0/1/2/3 is a poor input. It tells the model that steam (2)
  sits "between" chilled water (1) and hot water (3), and that hot water is
  "three times" electricity, which means nothing. Tree models can mostly work
  around this by splitting on it; an MLP can't.

  How to encode it
  - One-hot (4 binary columns). This is the default I'd use for the MLP. With
    only 4 categories it adds almost nothing to the input size, and the hidden
    layers can learn how meter type interacts with temperature and building
    features.
  - LightGBM / CatBoost: declare meter as a categorical feature rather than
    one-hot encoding it. These libraries handle categoricals natively and split
    on them better.
  - Embedding layer: not worth it for 4 values on its own. It becomes useful if
    you later embed building_id or a building_id × meter series ID, which has
    about 2,400 distinct values.

  How to structure the model around it (this matters more than the encoding)
  - Train a separate model per meter type. EDA shows the meters respond to
    weather in opposite directions: chilled water correlates +0.43 with
    temperature, while steam and hot water correlate about −0.45. Their scale
    and zero-reading patterns also differ. Separate models avoid all of that.
    The cost is 4 models to maintain, and less data for steam and hot water.
  - For the MLP, a middle ground: one shared network with 4 output heads, using
    the head that matches the row's meter. The heads share what's common, such
    as building size and time of day, but each meter gets its own output.
  - Interaction features, such as air_temperature × each one-hot meter column.
    These are only needed for linear or shallow models; an MLP or trees learn
    them on their own.

  A target-based feature
  - The mean log1p(meter_reading) per building_id × meter is probably the
    strongest single feature available, because it captures each meter's
    baseline level. It must be computed inside each cross-validation fold, or it
    leaks the target.

  My recommendation: one-hot encode meter for the MLP, pass it as categorical
  for gradient boosting, and compare a single pooled model against per-meter
  models in validation. I'd expect per-meter models or per-meter heads to win,
  given how different the meters behave in EDA.

❯ When we merge buildings with wether, for what exactly period of time we get
  weather. Give an example

⏺ I'll pull a concrete example from the merged data to show it.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
⏺ Each meter reading gets the weather for the same site at the exact same hour.
  The join key is (site_id, timestamp), so there's no averaging or time window.
  Weather is one row per site per hour, and every building at a site gets
  identical weather.

  Example from the merged data (buildings 0 and 1, both at site 0):

  ┌──────────┬────────┬────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────┐
  │ timestam │ buildi │ buildi │ air_temperat │ dew_temperat │ wind_spe │
  │    p     │ ng 0 r │ ng 1 r │     ure      │     ure      │    ed    │
  │          │ eading │ eading │              │              │          │
  ├──────────┼────────┼────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 2016-01- │ 0.0    │ 0.0    │ 25.0         │ 20.0         │ 0.0      │
  │ 01 00:00 │        │        │              │              │          │
  ├──────────┼────────┼────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 2016-01- │ 0.0    │ 0.0    │ 24.4         │ 21.1         │ 1.5      │
  │ 01 01:00 │        │        │              │              │          │
  ├──────────┼────────┼────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 2016-01- │ 0.0    │ 0.0    │ 22.8         │ 21.1         │ 0.0      │
  │ 01 02:00 │        │        │              │              │          │
  ├──────────┼────────┼────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 2016-01- │ 0.0    │ 0.0    │ 21.1         │ 20.6         │ 0.0      │
  │ 01 03:00 │        │        │              │              │          │
  └──────────┴────────┴────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────┘

  The reading at 01:00 gets site 0's 01:00 weather observation and nothing else.
  The weather values match the raw weather_train.csv row for 01:00 exactly.
  Hours that were missing from the raw file get the interpolated value for that
  hour instead.

  Two caveats:
  1. The timestamps may not refer to the same clock. 25 °C at midnight on 1
     January is odd, even for a warm site. It looks like 7 pm local time on a US
     east-coast site, which would mean the weather timestamps are in UTC while
     the meter timestamps are local time. The peak-temperature-hour check in EDA
     §7 tests exactly this. If each site's peak hour isn't around 14:00–15:00,
     the weather needs shifting by that site's UTC offset before the merge;
     otherwise each reading is paired with weather from several hours away.
  2. An hour-exact match ignores lag. Buildings react to weather with a delay,
     because thermal mass means walls heat and cool slowly. The 13:00 heating
     load depends on the morning's temperatures as well as 13:00's. That's a
     feature-engineering step rather than a merge fix: add lagged or rolling
     weather features, such as the mean temperature over the previous 6 or 24
     hours, computed per site.

❯ How it gets weather at exact same hour, if there is no timestamp in
  weather_train.csv

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ weather_train.csv does have a timestamp column; it's the second column in the
  file:

  site_id,timestamp,air_temperature,cloud_coverage,dew_temperature,...
  0,2016-01-01 00:00:00,25.0,6.0,20.0,,1019.7,0.0,0.0
  0,2016-01-01 01:00:00,24.4,,21.1,-1.0,1020.2,70.0,1.5

  The data description you pasted lists the weather columns starting from
  site_id and leaves out timestamp, which is likely where the confusion came
  from. The actual file has one row per site per hour. The merge joins on
  site_id and timestamp together, which is how each meter reading gets its own
  site's weather for the matching hour.

❯ I have an idea: for different meters we share the same weather data (I guesss
  so, how to verify?). Will it be architecturaly suitable to merge few rows
  with different meters and "target" into 1 row with different target for each
  metter?

⏺ The weather join key is (site_id, timestamp), and meter isn't part of it, so
  every meter of a building should get the same weather at a given hour. I'll
  check that on the merged data and see how many targets a wide
  one-row-per-building-hour table would leave empty.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Your guess is right. In 4,836,005 building-hours that have more than one
  meter, the meters never get different weather: 0 mismatches. That's guaranteed
  by the join, since the key is (site_id, timestamp) and doesn't include meter.
  To check it yourself, group the merged table by building_id and timestamp and
  confirm every weather column has only one distinct value per group, which is
  what I ran.

  Merging to one row per building-hour, with a target column per meter:

  ┌────────────────────┬────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │                    │ Long (current) │ Wide (your idea) │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Rows               │ 20.2M          │ 12.4M            │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Empty target cells │ 0              │ 59%              │
  └────────────────────┴────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  In the wide table, 7.56M rows have only 1 meter, 1.96M have 2, 2.77M have 3,
  and only 107K have all 4. Most buildings have one meter, so most target cells
  would be empty.

  For the MLP it's a good fit, with one required change.
  - It's the multi-head setup from earlier: one shared network with 4 outputs,
    trained on all meters at once.
  - The shared features are stored once per building-hour instead of repeated
    per meter.
  - The network can learn relationships between meters, for example a building
    that uses a lot of steam tends to use less hot water.
  - Required change: a masked loss. Compute the error only on target cells that
    exist, otherwise the model trains on the 59% empty cells. That's a few lines
    in PyTorch: multiply the squared errors by a 0/1 mask and divide by the
    mask's sum.

  For gradient boosting it doesn't help. LightGBM predicts one target at a time,
  so you'd train 4 models on the wide table. That's the same as training per
  meter on the long table, with extra reshaping.

  Two practical costs:
  - The submission is per row_id (building × meter × hour), so the test
    predictions have to be converted back to long format and matched to row_id.
  - When you split data for validation, split by time or by building, not by
    row. Wide rows don't change this, but it's easy to get wrong while
    reshaping.

  My recommendation: keep the saved Parquet in long format, since it serves both
  model types. Pivot to wide only inside the MLP's data loading step, and
  unpivot its predictions at the end.

⏺ I created mlp_test.ipynb (Homeworks/Homework%201/mlp_test.ipynb) and ran it
  end-to-end. The MLP reaches a validation RMSLE of 1.558, against 2.049 for a
  baseline that predicts each meter type's average. The whole run takes about 12
  seconds on your Mac's GPU (MPS).

  The decisions for this run are listed at the top of the notebook:
  - Target and loss: trained on log1p(meter_reading) with MSE, which is the same
    as optimizing RMSLE.
  - Validation: trained on January–September, validated on October–December.
  - Sample: 3M training rows and 1M validation rows, drawn at random.
  - Features: 33 in total, including one-hot meter and primary_use,
    log1p(square_feet), weather, and hour and day of week as sin/cos.
  - Model: 2 hidden ReLU layers of 64 units, built with nn.Sequential, trained
    with Adam for 5 epochs.

  ┌──────────────┬──────────┬───────┐
  │    meter     │ baseline │  MLP  │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────┤
  │ electricity  │ 1.556    │ 1.187 │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────┤
  │ chilledwater │ 2.631    │ 1.959 │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────┤
  │ steam        │ 2.544    │ 1.932 │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────┤
  │ hotwater     │ 2.629    │ 2.111 │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────┤
  │ all          │ 2.049    │ 1.558 │
  └──────────────┴──────────┴───────┘

  What the numbers say:
  - Validation stops improving after epoch 2 (1.566 at epoch 2, 1.558 at
    epoch 5), while the training score keeps improving. More epochs or a bigger
    network won't help much. The limit is the features.
  - The biggest missing piece is building identity. Two buildings with the same
    size, use and weather can differ in consumption by orders of magnitude, and
    nothing in the current features tells them apart. The next step with the
    most potential is an embedding for building_id × meter, or its mean log1p
    reading computed on the training data.
  - The long zero-reading runs are still in, so part of the error comes from
    broken or switched-off meters rather than real consumption. Removing them
    from training is the second step I'd try.

❯ Explain why the width of each layer is 64

⏺ Honestly, 64 was a default I picked, not a tuned value. It's a reasonable
  starting point for these reasons:

  - The lecture MLP used 32. I doubled it because this task has more inputs (33
    features against the lecture's small toy data) and far more training rows.
  - A common rule of thumb is a hidden width around 1–4× the input size. With 33
    inputs, 64 sits in that range.
  - There's no overfitting risk at this size. The network has about 6,400
    parameters (33×64 + 64×64 + 64, plus biases) against 3M training rows, so it
    can't memorize the data.
  - Powers of 2 are just a convention. They can map slightly better onto
    hardware, but 50 or 70 would work the same.

  The run suggests width isn't what's holding the model back. Validation RMSLE
  stopped improving after epoch 2 while training RMSLE kept falling. That
  pattern means the model has run out of useful signal in the features, not out
  of capacity. A wider network would mostly fit the training data more closely
  without improving validation.

  To choose the width properly, try 32, 64, 128 and 256 with everything else
  fixed and compare validation RMSLE. I'd expect little difference until
  building identity is added as a feature. Once there's a building_id embedding,
  the extra information per building could make more width worth having.

  ⎿  Updated cell features-md:
         1 ## 2. Features
         2
         3 Each raw column is turned into one or more numeric inputs:
         4
         5 | Raw column | Processing | Model inputs | Why |
         6 |---|---|---|---|
         7 | `meter` | one-hot | `meter_0` … `meter_3` | Codes 0–3 are ca
           tegories, not amounts. |
         8 | `primary_use` | one-hot | `use_*` (16 columns) | Categorical
           , 16 values. |
         9 | `square_feet` | `log1p` | `log_square_feet` | Heavily right-
           skewed; consumption grows with size on a log scale. |
        10 | `year_built` | fill NaN with the train median + flag | `year
           _built`, `year_built_missing` | 60% missing; the flag lets the
            model tell real values from filled ones. |
        11 | `floor_count` | dropped | — | 83% missing. |
        12 | `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature`, `sea_level_pressure`,
           `wind_speed` | as is | same names | Already numeric; gaps fill
           ed in data aggregation. |
        13 | `wind_direction` | sin/cos of the angle | `wind_dir_sin`, `w
           ind_dir_cos` | Keeps 359° next to 1°. |
        14 | `timestamp` | hour and day of week, each as sin/cos | `hour_
           sin`, `hour_cos`, `dow_sin`, `dow_cos` | Daily and weekly cycl
           es; 23:00 stays next to 00:00. |
        15 | `building_id`, `site_id` | not used | — | Left out of this f
           irst run. |
        16
        17 Then:
        18
        19 1. **Scaling.** `StandardScaler` rescales every input to mean
           0 and standard deviation 1. It is fitted on the train
        20    rows only and applied to both train and validation. Inputs
           on very different scales (pressure around 1000,
        21    one-hot columns 0/1) make gradient descent slow and unstabl
           e.
        22 2. **Target.** `y = log1p(meter_reading)`. Predictions are mad
           e in log space and clipped at 0 when scoring.
        23 3. **Tensors.** Features and targets are converted to `float32
           ` tensors and moved to the training device once, so
        24    each batch is just an index lookup during training.

⏺ I added an explanation to the "2. Features" section of mlp_test.ipynb
  (Homeworks/Homework%201/mlp_test.ipynb).

  - Feature table: for every raw column it shows how it's processed, which model
    inputs it becomes, and why. That covers the one-hot encodings,
    log1p(square_feet), the year_built median fill plus missing flag, and
    sin/cos for wind direction, hour and day of week. It also lists floor_count
    as dropped and building_id/site_id as unused.
  - Final steps: three short points cover StandardScaler (fitted on the training
    rows only), the log1p target clipped at 0 when scoring, and converting
    everything to float32 tensors on the training device once.

         7   hidden unit's outputs to mean 0 and standard deviation 1 wit

           o the learning rate.

  ⎿  Updated cell train:
         1 @torch.no_grad()
         2 def predict(model, X):
         3     model.eval()
         4     return (
         5         torch.cat([model(X[i : i + 65536]).squeeze(1) for i in
            range(0, len(X), 65536)])
         6         .cpu()
         7         .numpy()
         8     )
         9
        10
        11 def fit(model):
        12     optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=1e-3)
        13     loss_fn = nn.MSELoss()
        14     torch.manual_seed(0)
        15     history = []
        16     n = len(X_train_t)
        17     for epoch in range(1, EPOCHS + 1):
        18         model.train()
        19         perm = torch.randperm(n, device=device)
        20         total = torch.zeros((), device=device)
        21         for i in range(0, n, BATCH):
        22             idx = perm[i : i + BATCH]
        23             loss = loss_fn(model(X_train_t[idx]).squeeze(1), y
           _train_t[idx])
        24             optimizer.zero_grad()
        25             loss.backward()
        26             optimizer.step()
        27             total += loss.detach() * len(idx)
        28
        29         train_rmsle = (total / n).sqrt().item()
        30         val_rmsle = rmsle(y_val, predict(model, X_val_t))
        31         history.append((train_rmsle, val_rmsle))
        32         print(f"epoch {epoch} | train RMSLE {train_rmsle:.4f}
           | val RMSLE {val_rmsle:.4f}")
        33     return history
        34
        35
        36 models = {"mlp": mlp, "mlp_bn": mlp_bn}
        37 histories = {}
        38 for name, model in models.items():
        39     print(name)
        40     histories[name] = fit(model)

         1 # Homework 1 — MLP test run
         2
         3 A first MLP baseline trained on `data/processed/train_merged.p
           arquet` (built in [data_aggregation.ipynb](data_aggregation.ip
           ynb)).
         4
         5 ## Decisions for this run
         6
         7 1. **Format:** long (one row per building × meter × hour), one
            output. No wide/multi-head yet.
         8 2. **Target:** `log1p(meter_reading)` with MSE loss, so the RM
           SE of this loss is the competition's RMSLE.
         9 3. **Validation:** time split. Train on Jan–Sep 2016, validate
            on Oct–Dec 2016. A random split would put
        10    neighbouring hours of the same series in both sets.
        11 4. **Sample:** 3M train rows and 1M validation rows, drawn at
           random, to keep the run fast.
        12 5. **Features:**
        13    - `meter` and `primary_use`: one-hot.
        14    - `square_feet`: `log1p`.
        15    - `year_built`: median fill (from train) + missing flag. `f
           loor_count` dropped (83% missing).
        16    - Weather: `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature`, `sea_level
           _pressure`, `wind_speed` as is;
        17      `wind_direction` as sin/cos.
        18    - Time: hour of day and day of week as sin/cos. No month: v
           alidation months never appear in training.
        19    - Not used: `building_id`, `site_id`, `timestamp` itself.
        20 6. **Scaling:** `StandardScaler` on all features, fitted on tr
           ain only.
        21 7. **Data cleaning:** none. Long zero runs (EDA §6) stay in, s
           o the score includes them.
        22 8. **Models:** same shape as the lecture MLP — 2 hidden ReLU l
           ayers — built with `torch.nn`, 64 units each,
        23    linear output. Trained twice: plain, and with `BatchNorm1d`

❯ Implement 1 more model with 4 outputs instead of 1

⏺ The 4 outputs will share one trunk (the BatchNorm MLP, since it scored best).
  Each row is scored only on the output that matches its meter type. This needs
  no reshaping: the model returns 4 values per row, and I pick the right one
  using the row's meter code.
  ⎿  Updated cell model-md:
         1 ## 4. Models and training
         2
         3 Three models with the same hidden layers (2 × 64, ReLU):
         4
         5 - **`mlp`** — Linear → ReLU → Linear → ReLU → Linear, one outp
           ut.
         6 - **`mlp_bn`** — same, with `BatchNorm1d` after each hidden Li
           near layer (before ReLU). BatchNorm rescales each
         7   hidden unit's outputs to mean 0 and standard deviation 1 wit
           hin the batch, then applies a learned scale and
         8   shift. It usually makes training faster and less sensitive t
           o the learning rate.
         9 - **`mlp_4out`** — the `mlp_bn` hidden layers with **4 outputs
           , one per meter type**. The hidden layers are shared
        10   by all meters; each output only learns its own meter's patte
           rn (e.g. chilled water rising with temperature,
        11   steam falling). For each row, only the output matching its `
           meter` is used, both in the loss and in predictions,
        12   so the data stays in long format.
        13
        14 All use the same seed, optimizer (Adam, lr 1e-3), batch size (
           4096) and number of epochs (5).

  ⎿  Updated cell model:
         1 D = X_train_t.shape[1]
         2 EPOCHS = 5
         3 BATCH = 4096
         4
         5 M_train_t = torch.tensor(train_df.meter.to_numpy(), dtype=torc
           h.long, device=device)
         6 M_val_t = torch.tensor(val_df.meter.to_numpy(), dtype=torch.lo
           ng, device=device)
         7
         8
         9 class MultiOutputMLP(nn.Module):
        10     def __init__(self, d):
        11         super().__init__()
        12         self.hidden = nn.Sequential(
        13             nn.Linear(d, 64),
        14             nn.BatchNorm1d(64),
        15             nn.ReLU(),
        16             nn.Linear(64, 64),
        17             nn.BatchNorm1d(64),
        18             nn.ReLU(),
        19         )
        20         self.out = nn.Linear(64, 4)
        21
        22     def forward(self, x, meter):
        23         # 4 predictions per row; keep the one for the row's me
           ter type
        24         return self.out(self.hidden(x)).gather(1, meter.unsque
           eze(1)).squeeze(1)
        25
        26
        27 torch.manual_seed(0)
        28 mlp = nn.Sequential(
        29     nn.Linear(D, 64),
        30     nn.ReLU(),
        31     nn.Linear(64, 64),
        32     nn.ReLU(),
        33     nn.Linear(64, 1),
        34 ).to(device)
        35
        36 torch.manual_seed(0)
        37 mlp_bn = nn.Sequential(
        38     nn.Linear(D, 64),
        39     nn.BatchNorm1d(64),
        40     nn.ReLU(),
        41     nn.Linear(64, 64),
        42     nn.BatchNorm1d(64),
        43     nn.ReLU(),
        44     nn.Linear(64, 1),
        45 ).to(device)
        46
        47 torch.manual_seed(0)
        48 mlp_4out = MultiOutputMLP(D).to(device)
        49
        50 mlp_4out
  ⎿  Updated cell train:
         1 def run(model, X, M):
         2     if isinstance(model, MultiOutputMLP):
         3         return model(X, M)
         4     return model(X).squeeze(1)
         5
         6
         7 @torch.no_grad()
         8 def predict(model, X, M):
         9     model.eval()
        10     return (
        11         torch.cat([run(model, X[i : i + 65536], M[i : i + 6553
           6]) for i in range(0, len(X), 65536)])
        12         .cpu()
        13         .numpy()
        14     )
        15
        16
        17 def fit(model):
        18     optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=1e-3)
        19     loss_fn = nn.MSELoss()
        20     torch.manual_seed(0)
        21     history = []
        22     n = len(X_train_t)
        23     for epoch in range(1, EPOCHS + 1):
        24         model.train()
        25         perm = torch.randperm(n, device=device)
        26         total = torch.zeros((), device=device)
        27         for i in range(0, n, BATCH):
        28             idx = perm[i : i + BATCH]
        29             loss = loss_fn(run(model, X_train_t[idx], M_train_
           t[idx]), y_train_t[idx])
        30             optimizer.zero_grad()
        31             loss.backward()
        32             optimizer.step()
        33             total += loss.detach() * len(idx)
        34
        35         train_rmsle = (total / n).sqrt().item()
        36         val_rmsle = rmsle(y_val, predict(model, X_val_t, M_val
           _t))
        37         history.append((train_rmsle, val_rmsle))
        38         print(f"epoch {epoch} | train RMSLE {train_rmsle:.4f}
           | val RMSLE {val_rmsle:.4f}")
        39     return history
        40
        41
        42 models = {"mlp": mlp, "mlp_bn": mlp_bn, "mlp_4out": mlp_4out}
        43 histories = {}
        44 for name, model in models.items():
        45     print(name)
        46     histories[name] = fit(model)

         1 # Homework 1 — MLP test run
         2
         3 A first MLP baseline trained on `data/processed/train_merged.p
           arquet` (built in [data_aggregation.ipynb](data_aggregation.ip
           ynb)).
         4
         5 ## Decisions for this run
         6
         7 1. **Format:** long (one row per building × meter × hour). No
           pivot to one row per building-hour.
         8 2. **Target:** `log1p(meter_reading)` with MSE loss, so the RM
           SE of this loss is the competition's RMSLE.
         9 3. **Validation:** time split. Train on Jan–Sep 2016, validate
            on Oct–Dec 2016. A random split would put
        10    neighbouring hours of the same series in both sets.
        11 4. **Sample:** 3M train rows and 1M validation rows, drawn at
           random, to keep the run fast.
        12 5. **Features:**
        13    - `meter` and `primary_use`: one-hot.
        14    - `square_feet`: `log1p`.
        15    - `year_built`: median fill (from train) + missing flag. `f
           loor_count` dropped (83% missing).
        16    - Weather: `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature`, `sea_level
           _pressure`, `wind_speed` as is;
        17      `wind_direction` as sin/cos.
        18    - Time: hour of day and day of week as sin/cos. No month: v
           alidation months never appear in training.
        19    - Not used: `building_id`, `site_id`, `timestamp` itself.
        20 6. **Scaling:** `StandardScaler` on all features, fitted on tr
           ain only.
        21 7. **Data cleaning:** none. Long zero runs (EDA §6) stay in, s
           o the score includes them.
        22 8. **Models:** same shape as the lecture MLP — 2 hidden ReLU l
           ayers — built with `torch.nn`, 64 units each.
        23    Three variants: plain (1 output), with `BatchNorm1d` after
           each hidden layer (1 output), and with BatchNorm and
        24    4 outputs, one per meter type. Adam, lr 1e-3, batch 4096, 5
            epochs.

⏺ I added a third model, mlp_4out, to mlp_test.ipynb
  (Homeworks/Homework%201/mlp_test.ipynb) and ran it with the other two. It
  scores best of the three: validation RMSLE 1.524, against 1.541 for the
  BatchNorm model and 1.558 for the plain MLP.

  How it works: it uses the same hidden layers as the BatchNorm model, but the
  final layer produces 4 values, one per meter type. For each row, only the
  value matching its meter is used, both in the loss and in the prediction. The
  data stays in long format, and each output is trained only on its own meter's
  rows.

  ┌──────────────┬──────────┬───────┬────────┬──────────┐
  │    meter     │ baseline │  mlp  │ mlp_bn │ mlp_4out │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────┼────────┼──────────┤
  │ electricity  │ 1.556    │ 1.187 │ 1.174  │ 1.168    │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────┼────────┼──────────┤
  │ chilledwater │ 2.631    │ 1.959 │ 1.942  │ 1.915    │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────┼────────┼──────────┤
  │ steam        │ 2.544    │ 1.932 │ 1.921  │ 1.894    │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────┼────────┼──────────┤
  │ hotwater     │ 2.629    │ 2.111 │ 2.052  │ 2.024    │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────┼────────┼──────────┤
  │ all          │ 2.049    │ 1.558 │ 1.541  │ 1.524    │
  └──────────────┴──────────┴───────┴────────┴──────────┘

  - Where the gain comes from: compared with the 1-output BatchNorm model, the
    improvement is about 0.027 on each heating and cooling meter but only 0.006
    on electricity. That fits the idea that meters which react to weather in
    opposite directions benefit most from having their own output.
  - Each step is small: about 0.02 overall, from a single seed, so repeating the
    runs with a few seeds would confirm the ranking.

  The "Decisions" list, the models section and the written comparison in the
  notebook now cover all three models.
