❯ Are these RMSEs trustworhy? Provide the ideas for careful validation. what's
  about current?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The scores are good enough for a rough ranking against the baseline. They
  aren't reliable for the 0.02 gaps between the three models, and they're
  probably not a good estimate of the score on the competition's test set.

  What the current setup gets right

  - It splits by time (train January–September, validate October–December), so
    validation rows come after all training rows. A random split would score
    much better, because neighbouring hours of the same meter would sit in both
    sets.
  - Nothing is fitted on validation data. The scaler, the year_built median and
    the baseline all use training rows only.
  - All models are scored on the same 1M validation rows, so the comparison
    between them is fair.
  - Using weather from the validation period is fine. The competition provides
    weather_test.csv, so real test-time predictions have that weather too.

  Where it can mislead

  1. Validation covers only autumn and early winter. The competition's test set
     spans 2017–2018, full years. October–December is mostly heating season, so
     the summer cooling load for chilled water is barely tested. The score may
     not reflect full-year performance.
  2. The validation set also picks the winner. I compared three models and
     reported each one's last epoch, both on the same October–December data.
     With small gaps, that makes the winner's score slightly optimistic.
  3. Each model ran with one seed. The 0.017 gaps between models are the same
     size as the epoch-to-epoch swings: the plain MLP moved between 1.558 and
     1.574 across epochs. We don't know how much of the gap is noise.
  4. 1M rows is not 1M independent samples. Rows come from about 2,000 meter
     series, and readings from the same meter over time are strongly correlated.
     The real uncertainty depends mainly on which buildings are in validation,
     and it's larger than the row count suggests.
  5. A few broken meters may drive the score. Long zero-reading runs (broken or
     switched-off meters, EDA §6) are still in validation. Missing a reading of
     0 by a large prediction costs a lot in log space, so a handful of broken
     meters could decide which model "wins".
  6. Only one 3M-row training sample was used. A different random sample could
     move the scores somewhat.

  Ideas for more careful validation

  Split scheme
  - Rolling time folds: for example, train Jan–Mar → validate Apr–May, train
    Jan–May → validate Jun–Jul, and so on. Report the mean and spread across
    folds; this covers both heating and cooling seasons.
  - Split by building (group split on building_id) as a second check. It tests
    how well the model handles buildings it has never seen. The competition's
    test set uses the same buildings as training, so the time split is the main
    measure; this is a robustness check.
  - A final held-out period (e.g. December) that is never used for model or
    epoch selection, and is scored only once at the end.

  Measuring noise
  - Run each model with 3–5 seeds and report mean ± standard deviation. A gap
    smaller than the spread isn't a real difference.
  - Resample whole buildings with replacement (a bootstrap over building_id) to
    get a confidence interval, and check whether one model beats another in most
    resamples.
  - Keep the best epoch, not the last. Pick it on a separate validation slice so
    it doesn't inflate the reported score.

  Understanding where the error comes from
  - Report the score with and without the long zero-reading runs, so it's clear
    how much of the error is broken meters and how much is real consumption.
  - Break the score down by meter, site and month, which shows whether one site
    or one season dominates.
  - Adversarial validation: train a classifier to tell training rows from
    validation rows; features it relies on have shifted between the periods. The
    course's Validation_guide.md (Development%20Guide/Validation_guide.md) §6
    describes this.

  The biggest single improvement would be rolling time folds plus several seeds.
  Once those two are in place, a ranking between models can be trusted, and
  small gaps like 0.017 can be confirmed or ruled out.

  submission to data/processed/submission_mlp_4out.csv. The whole notebook runs
  in about 1 minute.

  The test set has no labels, so there's no score yet; the real score only comes
  from submitting to Kaggle.

  What the new section does:
  - Predicts all 41,697,600 test rows in chunks of 2M. It uses the scaler and
    year_built median fitted on the training data, so nothing is fitted on test.
  - Converts site 0 electricity back to kBTU. That's 1,839,600 rows. The model
    was trained on these readings in kWh, but Kaggle scores them in the original
    units.

    same order, and there are no negative or missing predictions.

  ├──────────────┼───────┼──────────────────┤

  The differences are what you'd expect: the test set covers full years,

  hot water predictions are higher and chilled water lower.

  its Kaggle score will probably be worse than a version retrained on the full
  year.

  "data/processed/submission_mlp_4out.csv" -m "mlp_4out baseline"
  The file is 711 MB, so zipping it first makes the upload much faster.

❯ Provide ideas for further improvement of the model

⏺ Ordered roughly by expected impact, starting with the biggest:

  1. Tell the model which building it's looking at
  - This is the largest gap. The model can't tell apart two buildings with the
    same size, use and weather, and their consumption can differ by orders of
    magnitude.
  - Option A: an embedding for building_id × meter (about 2,400 series), with
    8–16 dimensions, fed into the hidden layers.
  - Option B: simple statistics per series from the training data, such as the
    mean log1p reading, or its mean by hour of week.
  - Either one should improve the score far more than any architecture change so
    far.

  2. Remove broken-meter data from training
  - Drop the long zero-reading runs (EDA §6) from the training set, especially
    for electricity. Site 0 electricity is zero for several months of
    early 2016.
  - Right now the model is learning to predict broken meters, which pulls every
    other prediction down.
  - Keep scoring validation both with and without these rows, so you can see the
    effect.

  3. Fix the weather alignment
  - The midnight temperature of 25 °C at site 0 suggests the weather timestamps
    are UTC while the meter timestamps are local time.
  - Check each site's peak-temperature hour (EDA §7) and shift that site's
    weather by its UTC offset before merging.
  - Otherwise every reading is paired with weather from several hours away.

  4. Better weather features
  - Lagged and rolling weather, e.g. the mean temperature over the previous 6
    and 24 hours per site. Buildings heat and cool slowly, so recent weather
    matters as well as the current hour.
  - Heating and cooling degree-hours: how far the temperature is below 18 °C and
    above 22 °C (common base temperatures; tune them). This matches how heating
    and cooling load actually respond.
  - Relative humidity, computed from air temperature and dew point. Humidity
    drives air-conditioning load.

  5. Calendar features
  - A US holidays flag. Most sites are university and office buildings, and
    holidays cause low-consumption days that hour and day of week can't explain.
  - Once validation covers all seasons, add day of year or month as well.

  6. Use more data and train better
  - Train on all 20M rows instead of 3M, for more epochs, with a learning-rate
    schedule.
  - Keep the best epoch using a separate validation slice (early stopping).
  - For the final model, retrain on the full year.

  7. Architecture, once the features above are in
  - A wider network (128–256) with dropout. With building embeddings there's
    much more to learn, so extra width is likely to pay off then, though not
    before.
  - Try the wide format with 4 outputs trained at once and a masked loss, as
    discussed earlier.

  8. Compare against LightGBM, then combine
  - Gradient-boosted trees are very strong on tabular data like this. A LightGBM
    model with the same features gives an honest reference point.
  - Averaging the MLP and LightGBM predictions usually beats either one alone.

  Before all of this: fix validation. Use rolling time folds and several seeds,
  so you can tell whether each change is a real improvement or noise. The gaps
  so far (about 0.02 RMSLE) are within the noise of the current setup.

  If you want to pick one to start with, I'd do 1 and 2 together. They're the
  most likely to cut the score substantially.

❯ /model sonnet
  ⎿  Set model to claude-sonnet-5

❯ What is the sense of adding embedings?

⏺ An embedding turns a category (here, building_id, about 1,450 of them) into a
  small vector of numbers that the model learns during training — instead of
  one-hot encoding, which would add 1,450 mostly-empty columns, or leaving
  building_id out entirely, which is what we did.

  Why it's needed here. Right now the model only sees size, use, weather and
  time. But two buildings with identical square footage, primary use, and
  weather can consume wildly different amounts of energy — because of insulation
  quality, occupancy schedule, whether it has a data center, how old the HVAC
  equipment is, and dozens of other things not in the data at all. None of that
  is recoverable from the features we do have. Let me check how much variance
  actually exists between same-profile buildings.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ That confirms it concretely. Take 20 Education buildings, all between
  20,000–25,000 sq ft — same primary_use, same size bucket, same weather station
  coverage. Their mean electricity readings range from 0.5 to 125.2, a 250×
  spread, with the model's current features unable to distinguish any of them.
  The model has to predict one number for all 20, because size/use/weather is
  all it's given, and lands somewhere in the middle — wrong for nearly everyone.

  What an embedding does about it. Give each building_id (or building_id ×
  meter) a learnable vector of, say, 8 numbers, initialized randomly. During
  training, backpropagation adjusts each building's vector so that whatever the
  network does with it helps predict that specific building's actual readings.
  It's a per-building "fingerprint" the network invents on its own, from the
  meter_reading data — including this 0.5–125 spread the raw features can never
  explain.

  Why not just one-hot encode building_id?
  - One-hot gives 1,450 independent columns with no relationship between them —
    building 501 and building 502 are unrelated categories. An embedding lets
    the network place similar buildings near each other in the vector space,
    e.g. it might discover that buildings 501 and 502 behave alike, purely from
    data, without being told.
  - It's compact: 8-16 numbers per building vs. 1,450 columns.
  - It generalizes better with sparse data (many buildings have limited
    history).

  The catch: it's not a free improvement, it's closer to memorization. An
  embedding only knows about buildings it saw during training. Since this
  competition's test set reuses the same buildings as train (same building_ids,
  different time period), that's fine here — the embedding for building 501
  learned from January–September still applies in the test period. But it would
  be useless for a genuinely new building never seen before; there's nothing for
  the model to look up. This is worth flagging as the main tradeoff versus the
  size/use/weather features, which generalize to any building.

  Simpler alternative to a full embedding layer: compute each building×meter's
  mean log1p(meter_reading) on the training data and feed that in as a single
  number instead of a learned vector. It captures most of the same information —
  the building's baseline level — with less code and no training instability
  risk. I'd try that first, and reach for a real embedding only if it's not
  enough.

❯ What does rmlse penalizes for mostly?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ RMSLE penalizes relative error, not absolute error, on both sides of the log —
  but that symmetry breaks down completely near zero.

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────┐
  │                  case                  │        error        │  RMSLE   │
  │                                        │                     │   cost   │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 100 → 110                              │ +10%                │ 0.094    │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 10,000 → 11,000                        │ +10%                │ 0.095    │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 10,000 → 11,000                        │ +1,000 absolute     │ 0.095    │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 100 → 1,100                            │ +1,000 absolute     │ 2.389    │
  │                                        │ (+1000%)            │          │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ truth 0, predict 5                     │ —                   │ 1.792    │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ truth 0, predict 50                    │ —                   │ 3.932    │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ truth 50, predict 0 (miss a real       │ —                   │ 3.932    │
  │ reading entirely)                      │                     │          │
  └────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────┘

  The main thing it penalizes: getting the ratio wrong, at any scale. A 10% miss
  costs the same 0.095 whether the true value is 100 or 10,000 — this is the
  whole point of using log1p, and why it fits a target like meter_reading that
  spans several orders of magnitude across meter types and buildings.

  What it actually punishes hardest, in practice: rows near zero. Predicting 5
  when the truth is 0 costs more (1.79) than a 10% miss on a huge reading
  (0.095) — it's off by a factor of ∞ in relative terms, even though it's off by
  only 5 units in absolute terms. And missing a real non-zero reading with a
  prediction of 0 costs the most of all (3.93) — that's the single worst thing
  you can do under this metric.

  Why this matters for this dataset specifically: the long zero-reading runs
  from broken/switched-off meters (EDA §6) sit exactly in this danger zone. A
  model that's slightly too confident and predicts a small positive number
  during an outage gets penalized far out of proportion to how "wrong" that
  error looks in real units — which is the whole reason I recommended earlier
  that outage rows probably shouldn't just be trained on unfiltered, and that
  RMSLE-with-vs-without-zero-runs is worth reporting separately.

  ⎿  Updated cell intro:
         1 # Homework 1 — MLP test run
         2
         3 A first MLP baseline trained on `data/processed/train_merged.p
           arquet` (built in [data_aggregation.ipynb](data_aggregation.ip
           ynb)).
         4
         5 ## Decisions for this run
         6
         7 1. **Format:** long (one row per building × meter × hour). No
           pivot to one row per building-hour.
         8 2. **Target:** `log1p(meter_reading)` with MSE loss, so the RM
           SE of this loss is the competition's RMSLE.
         9 3. **Validation:** time split. Train on Jan–Sep 2016, validate
            on Oct–Dec 2016. A random split would put
        10    neighbouring hours of the same series in both sets.
        11 4. **Sample:** 3M train rows and 1M validation rows, drawn at
           random, to keep the run fast.
        12 5. **Features:**
        13    - `meter` and `primary_use`: one-hot.
        14    - `square_feet`: `log1p`.
        15    - `year_built`: median fill (from train) + missing flag. `f
           loor_count` dropped (83% missing).
        16    - Weather: `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature`, `sea_level
           _pressure`, `wind_speed` as is;
        17      `wind_direction` as sin/cos.
        18    - Time: hour of day and day of week as sin/cos. No month: v
           alidation months never appear in training.
        19    - Not used: `building_id`, `site_id`, `timestamp` itself.
        20 6. **Scaling:** `StandardScaler` on all features, fitted on tr
           ain only.
        21 7. **Data cleaning:** none. Long zero runs (EDA §6) stay in, s
           o the score includes them.
        22 8. **Models:** same shape as the lecture MLP — 2 hidden ReLU l
           ayers — built with `torch.nn`, 64 units each.
        23    Three variants: plain (1 output), with `BatchNorm1d` after
           each hidden layer (1 output), and with BatchNorm and
        24    4 outputs, one per meter type. Adam, lr 1e-3, batch 4096, 5
            epochs.
        25 9. **Reference model:** LightGBM on the same features and rows
           , untuned (500 trees, lr 0.1, 63 leaves).

⏺ I trained LightGBM on the same features and rows as the MLPs and added it to
  mlp_test.ipynb (Homeworks/Homework%201/mlp_test.ipynb). It clearly beats all
  of them: validation RMSLE 1.392, against 1.524 for the best MLP (mlp_4out).
  The notebook runs end-to-end in about 2 minutes.

  LightGBM was untuned (500 trees, learning rate 0.1, 63 leaves), with the same
  log1p target and MSE loss as the MLPs.

  │    meter     │ baseline │  mlp  │ mlp_bn │ mlp_4out │ lgbm  │

  └──────────────┴──────────┴───────┴────────┴──────────┴───────┘

  Why LightGBM wins: its most-used feature is floor area, with 3× the splits of
  any other. I checked, and 1,360 of the 1,449 buildings have a floor area no
  other building shares. So the trees can split on exact floor area to single
  out individual buildings, which gives them the building identity the MLPs
  don't have. The MLPs see floor area as one smooth scaled number and can't do
  that. This is the strongest evidence yet that adding building identity is the
  next step for the MLP.

  On electricity the two are level; the whole gap comes from the three heating
  and cooling meters, where LightGBM is 0.22–0.31 better.

  Two setup changes:
  - LightGBM is now a dependency. I added it with poetry add, which changed
    pyproject.toml and poetry.lock.
  - LightGBM runs single-threaded. With multiple threads it crashes the kernel
    whenever PyTorch has been used in the same process, because both load their
    own copy of the OpenMP library on macOS. The notebook cell has a comment
    explaining this. It runs a bit slower as a result, about 1.5 minutes for
    this dataset.

  The written comparison and the decisions list at the top of the notebook now
  include LightGBM. The submission file is still from mlp_4out; if you want, I
  can switch the test-set section to LightGBM, since it's the better model.

❯ Implement also embedings and its "simpler alternative", compare to mlp_4out
  and lgbm

⏺ I'll add both building-identity approaches on top of mlp_4out so the
  comparison isolates their effect:
  - Simpler alternative: one extra input, the mean log1p reading of each
    building × meter series on the training rows.
  - Embedding: a learned 16-number vector per building_id, joined to the inputs
    of the hidden layers. The 4 meter outputs then turn it into per-meter
    predictions.

  First, the model classes and the training helpers, which now also need the
  building ID.
  ⎿  Updated cell model:
         1 D = X_train_t.shape[1]
         2 EPOCHS = 5
         3 BATCH = 4096
         4
         5 M_train_t = torch.tensor(train_df.meter.to_numpy(), dtype=torc
           h.long, device=device)
         6 M_val_t = torch.tensor(val_df.meter.to_numpy(), dtype=torch.lo
           ng, device=device)
         7 B_train_t = torch.tensor(train_df.building_id.to_numpy(), dtyp
           e=torch.long, device=device)
         8 B_val_t = torch.tensor(val_df.building_id.to_numpy(), dtype=to
           rch.long, device=device)
         9
        10
        11 class MultiOutputMLP(nn.Module):
        12     def __init__(self, d):
        13         super().__init__()
        14         self.hidden = nn.Sequential(
        15             nn.Linear(d, 64),
        16             nn.BatchNorm1d(64),
        17             nn.ReLU(),
        18             nn.Linear(64, 64),
        19             nn.BatchNorm1d(64),
        20             nn.ReLU(),
        21         )
        22         self.out = nn.Linear(64, 4)
        23
        24     def forward(self, x, meter):
        25         # 4 predictions per row; keep the one for the row's me
           ter type
        26         return self.out(self.hidden(x)).gather(1, meter.unsque
           eze(1)).squeeze(1)
        27
        28
        29 class EmbeddingMLP(nn.Module):
        30     def __init__(self, d, n_buildings, emb_dim=16):
        31         super().__init__()
        32         self.emb = nn.Embedding(n_buildings, emb_dim)
        33         self.net = MultiOutputMLP(d + emb_dim)
        34
        35     def forward(self, x, meter, building):
        36         return self.net(torch.cat([x, self.emb(building)], dim
           =1), meter)
        37
        38
        39 torch.manual_seed(0)
        40 mlp = nn.Sequential(
        41     nn.Linear(D, 64),
        42     nn.ReLU(),
        43     nn.Linear(64, 64),
        44     nn.ReLU(),
        45     nn.Linear(64, 1),
        46 ).to(device)
        47
        48 torch.manual_seed(0)
        49 mlp_bn = nn.Sequential(
        50     nn.Linear(D, 64),
        51     nn.BatchNorm1d(64),
        52     nn.ReLU(),
        53     nn.Linear(64, 64),
        54     nn.BatchNorm1d(64),
        55     nn.ReLU(),
        56     nn.Linear(64, 1),
        57 ).to(device)
        58
        59 torch.manual_seed(0)
        60 mlp_4out = MultiOutputMLP(D).to(device)
        61
        62 mlp_4out
  ⎿  Updated cell train:
         1 def run(model, X, M, B):
         2     if isinstance(model, EmbeddingMLP):
         3         return model(X, M, B)
         4     if isinstance(model, MultiOutputMLP):
         5         return model(X, M)
         6     return model(X).squeeze(1)
         7
         8
         9 @torch.no_grad()
        10 def predict(model, X, M, B):
        11     model.eval()
        12     step = 65536
        13     return (
        14         torch.cat(
        15             [run(model, X[i : i + step], M[i : i + step], B[i
           : i + step]) for i in range(0, len(X), step)]
        16         )
        17         .cpu()
        18         .numpy()
        19     )
        20
        21
        22 def fit(model, X_tr=X_train_t, X_va=X_val_t):
        23     optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=1e-3)
        24     loss_fn = nn.MSELoss()
        25     torch.manual_seed(0)
        26     history = []
        27     n = len(X_tr)
        28     for epoch in range(1, EPOCHS + 1):
        29         model.train()
        30         perm = torch.randperm(n, device=device)
        31         total = torch.zeros((), device=device)
        32         for i in range(0, n, BATCH):
        33             idx = perm[i : i + BATCH]
        34             loss = loss_fn(run(model, X_tr[idx], M_train_t[idx
           ], B_train_t[idx]), y_train_t[idx])
        35             optimizer.zero_grad()
        36             loss.backward()
        37             optimizer.step()
        38             total += loss.detach() * len(idx)
        39
        40         train_rmsle = (total / n).sqrt().item()
        41         val_rmsle = rmsle(y_val, predict(model, X_va, M_val_t,
            B_val_t))
        42         history.append((train_rmsle, val_rmsle))
        43         print(f"epoch {epoch} | train RMSLE {train_rmsle:.4f}
           | val RMSLE {val_rmsle:.4f}")
        44     return history
        45
        46
        47 models = {"mlp": mlp, "mlp_bn": mlp_bn, "mlp_4out": mlp_4out}
        48 histories = {}

⏺ Edit Notebook(Homeworks/Homework 1/mlp_test.ipynb@271c93e5)
  ⎿  Updated cell fa9f60d2:
         1 ### Building identity
         2
         3 Two ways to tell the MLP which building a row comes from, both
            added on top of `mlp_4out` (BatchNorm, 4 outputs),
         4 so any difference comes from the building information alone:
         5
         6 - **`mlp_4out_mean`** — one extra input: the mean `log1p(meter
           _reading)` of the row's building × meter series,
         7   computed on the training rows only. A series with no trainin
           g rows gets its meter type's mean instead.
         8 - **`mlp_4out_emb`** — a learned vector of 16 numbers per `bui
           lding_id` (`nn.Embedding`), joined to the other
         9   inputs before the hidden layers. It starts random and is tra
           ined along with the rest of the network. It's per
        10   building rather than per building × meter, because the 4 met
           er outputs already handle the meter type.
        11
        12 Both only help for buildings seen in training, which holds her
           e: the competition's test set uses the same buildings.

         1 **Comparison**
         2
         3 - **BatchNorm vs plain:** BatchNorm is slightly better on ever
           y meter type (validation RMSLE 1.541 vs 1.558), and
         4   its validation score changes more smoothly between epochs.
         5 - **4 outputs vs 1 output** (both with BatchNorm): 4 outputs i
           s better again on every meter type
         6   (1.524 vs 1.541). The gain is about 0.027 on each heating/co
           oling meter (chilled water, steam, hot water) but
         7   only 0.006 on electricity. Heating and cooling react to weat
           her in opposite directions, so giving each meter its
         8   own output helps most there.
         9 - **LightGBM vs `mlp_4out`:** LightGBM is much better (1.392 v
           s 1.524). Its most-used feature is
        10   `log_square_feet`, and 1,360 of the 1,449 buildings have a f
           loor area no other building shares, so the trees can
        11   single out individual buildings by exact floor area. The MLP
            can't.

           ey gain 0.26–0.39 on the heating/cooling meters.

           h their best validation score after epoch 1
        17   (1.316 series mean, 1.303 embedding), then get worse while t
           he training score keeps falling (to 0.99 and 0.92).

  ⎿  Updated cell intro:
         1 # Homework 1 — MLP test run
         2
         3 A first MLP baseline trained on `data/processed/train_merged.p
           arquet` (built in [data_aggregation.ipynb](data_aggregation.ip
           ynb)).
         4
         5 ## Decisions for this run
         6
         7 1. **Format:** long (one row per building × meter × hour). No
           pivot to one row per building-hour.
         8 2. **Target:** `log1p(meter_reading)` with MSE loss, so the RM
           SE of this loss is the competition's RMSLE.
         9 3. **Validation:** time split. Train on Jan–Sep 2016, validate
            on Oct–Dec 2016. A random split would put
        10    neighbouring hours of the same series in both sets.
        11 4. **Sample:** 3M train rows and 1M validation rows, drawn at
           random, to keep the run fast.
        12 5. **Features:**
        13    - `meter` and `primary_use`: one-hot.
        14    - `square_feet`: `log1p`.
        15    - `year_built`: median fill (from train) + missing flag. `f
           loor_count` dropped (83% missing).
        16    - Weather: `air_temperature`, `dew_temperature`, `sea_level
           _pressure`, `wind_speed` as is;
        17      `wind_direction` as sin/cos.
        18    - Time: hour of day and day of week as sin/cos. No month: v
           alidation months never appear in training.
        19    - Not used: `site_id`, `timestamp` itself. `building_id` is
            used only by the building-identity models (item 9).
        20 6. **Scaling:** `StandardScaler` on all features, fitted on tr
           ain only.
        21 7. **Data cleaning:** none. Long zero runs (EDA §6) stay in, s
           o the score includes them.
        22 8. **Models:** same shape as the lecture MLP — 2 hidden ReLU l
           ayers — built with `torch.nn`, 64 units each.
        23    Three variants: plain (1 output), with `BatchNorm1d` after
           each hidden layer (1 output), and with BatchNorm and
        24    4 outputs, one per meter type. Adam, lr 1e-3, batch 4096, 5
            epochs.
        25 9. **Building identity:** the 4-output model plus either the p
           er-series mean `log1p` reading as an extra input, or
        26    a 16-dimensional `building_id` embedding.
        27 10. **Reference model:** LightGBM on the same features and row
           s, untuned (500 trees, lr 0.1, 63 leaves).

⏺ I added both building-identity models to mlp_test.ipynb
  (Homeworks/Homework%201/mlp_test.ipynb), built on top of mlp_4out, and ran the
  notebook end-to-end (about 2 minutes). Both now beat LightGBM. The simpler
  alternative scores best of all: 1.349, against 1.365 for the embedding and
  1.392 for LightGBM.

  ┌──────────────┬──────────┬───────────────┬──────────────┬───────┐
  │    meter     │ mlp_4out │ mlp_4out_mean │ mlp_4out_emb │ lgbm  │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┼───────┤
  │ electricity  │ 1.168    │ 1.160         │ 1.177        │ 1.178 │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┼───────┤
  │ chilledwater │ 1.915    │ 1.522         │ 1.512        │ 1.601 │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┼───────┤
  │ steam        │ 1.894    │ 1.584         │ 1.632        │ 1.671 │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┼───────┤
  │ hotwater     │ 2.024    │ 1.767         │ 1.788        │ 1.778 │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┼───────┤
  │ all          │ 1.524    │ 1.349         │ 1.365        │ 1.392 │
  └──────────────┴──────────┴───────────────┴──────────────┴───────┘

  What the two new models are:
  - mlp_4out_mean (the simpler alternative) gets one extra input: the mean log1p
    reading of the row's building × meter series, computed on the training rows
    only.
  - mlp_4out_emb learns a 16-number vector per building, joined to the inputs of
    the hidden layers.

  What the results show:
  - Building identity was the missing piece. It improves the heating and cooling
    meters by 0.24–0.40 and the overall score by 0.16–0.18. That's far bigger
    than any architecture change so far.
  - Both new models overfit to the training months. Their validation score is
    best after epoch 1 (1.316 for the series mean, 1.303 for the embedding) and
    gets worse after that, while the training score keeps falling to about
    0.9–1.0. They learn each building's January–September level, which doesn't
    fully carry over to October–December. Early stopping and training on data
    from every season should both help. I didn't report the epoch-1 scores as
    results, because picking them would use the validation set for the choice.
  - Electricity barely moves with any model (1.16–1.19). Its error probably
    comes mostly from the broken-meter zero runs, which none of these features
    can predict.

  The written comparison and the decisions list in the notebook are updated. The
  submission file is still from mlp_4out; switching it to mlp_4out_mean is the
  obvious next step, and I can do that if you want.

❯ How much % of the data is measuring electricity?
  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Electricity is 59.7% of the training rows (12,060,910 of 20,216,100).

  ┌──────────────┬────────────┬───────┐
  │    meter     │    rows    │   %   │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼───────┤
  │ electricity  │ 12,060,910 │ 59.7% │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼───────┤
  │ chilledwater │ 4,182,440  │ 20.7% │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼───────┤
  │ steam        │ 2,708,713  │ 13.4% │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼───────┤
  │ hotwater     │ 1,264,037  │ 6.3%  │
  └──────────────┴────────────┴───────┘

  Nearly every building has an electricity meter, while chilled water, steam and
  hot water only apply to buildings with that kind of HVAC system, which is why
  they're smaller. This also explains why a pooled single-output model tends to
  fit electricity's pattern best — it's the majority of the loss — and why the
  4-output split mattered less for electricity than for the other three: it
  already dominated what a shared model would learn anyway.

❯ Tell about broken meters for electricity

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ 4.4% of all electricity readings are exactly zero, but they're not spread
  evenly — they're concentrated in long, contiguous blocks, which is the
  signature of a broken or not-yet-installed meter rather than a building
  genuinely using zero electricity for a while (unlike heating/cooling meters,
  where a real off-season zero is expected).

  Run-length breakdown (12,751 separate zero-runs total):

  ┌────────────────────┬────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │ minimum run length │ # runs │ rows covered │ % of electricity │
  ├────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ ≥ 1 day            │ 1,860  │ 484,670      │ 4.0%             │
  ├────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ ≥ 1 week           │ 361    │ 410,032      │ 3.4%             │
  ├────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ ≥ 1 month          │ 123    │ 315,561      │ 2.6%             │
  ├────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ ≥ 3 months         │ 83     │ 263,308      │ 2.2%             │
  └────────────────────┴────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────┘

  So almost all of the zero mass (2.2 of the 4.0 percentage points) comes from
  runs lasting 3 months or longer — a small number of meters (83 runs) stuck at
  zero for a huge stretch of the year, not random dropouts. The five longest
  single runs are 220–310 days (5,290–7,404 hours) uninterrupted.

  It's concentrated at one site. Site 0 alone has 38.1% of its electricity
  readings at zero — miles above every other site (site 7 is next at 12.1%, then
  it drops to under 5%). This matches what showed up earlier: site 0 is also
  the site with the kBTU/kWh unit bug, suggesting its whole electricity pipeline
  had data problems for 2016. A typical example building at site 0 reads
  exactly zero from January 1 straight through May 20 — the meter (or its data
  feed) was simply offline for the first third of the year.

  Why this matters for the models: all four models trained so far include these
  rows unfiltered. Since RMSLE punishes a nonzero prediction against a true zero
  heavily (as discussed earlier), a model that's even slightly optimistic
  during site 0's 5-month outage pays a large, somewhat arbitrary penalty that
  has nothing to do with how well it models real consumption. This is very
  likely a real drag on the electricity RMSLE (which barely improved with any
  feature added, 1.16–1.19 across all models) — worth testing by scoring
  electricity with and without site 0's known outage period, and considering
  whether to exclude it from training entirely.

  ⎿  Updated cell 0465847f:
         1 <a id='broken'></a>
         2
         3 # 10. Baseline: effect of broken electricity meters
         4
         5 §6 and §9 suggest that long runs of zero electricity readings
           are broken or switched-off meters, and that they
         6 could dominate RMSLE. This section measures it with a LightGBM
            baseline.
         7
         8 - **Broken electricity** = an electricity series that reads ex
           actly 0 for at least 168 consecutive hourly readings
         9   (one week). A building in use never draws zero electricity f
           or a week, so these are meter or data-feed problems.
        10   Heating and cooling meters are not flagged: their long zero
           runs can be a real off-season.
        11 - **Data:** `data/processed/train_merged.parquet` from [data_a
           ggregation.ipynb](data_aggregation.ipynb) (site-0
        12   units fixed, weather gaps filled).
        13 - **Split:** train on Jan–Sep 2016, validate on Oct–Dec 2016.
           3M random training rows, 1M validation rows.
        14 - **Model:** LightGBM, untuned (500 trees, learning rate 0.1,
           63 leaves), `log1p` target with MSE loss. Features:
        15   `meter`, `primary_use`, `square_feet`, `year_built`, weather
           , hour, day of week.
        16 - **Two versions:** **A** trains on all rows; **B** trains wit
           hout the broken electricity rows. Both are scored on
        17   the full validation set and on subsets, to separate "predict
           ing broken meters" from "predicting consumption".

⏺ Edit Notebook(Homeworks/Homework 1/EDA.ipynb@0465847f)
  ⎿  Updated cell d6fccc62:
         1 data = (
         2     pd.read_parquet(DATA / "processed" / "train_merged.parquet
           ")
         3     .sort_values(["building_id", "meter", "timestamp"])
         4     .reset_index(drop=True)
         5 )
         6
         7 is_zero = data.meter_reading.eq(0)
         8 new_run = (
         9     is_zero.ne(is_zero.shift())
        10     | data.building_id.ne(data.building_id.shift())
        11     | data.meter.ne(data.meter.shift())
        12 )
        13 run_len = is_zero.groupby(new_run.cumsum()).transform("size")
        14 data["broken"] = is_zero & (run_len >= 168) & (data.meter == 0
           )
        15
        16 elec = data[data.meter == 0]
        17 print(f"broken electricity rows: {data.broken.sum():,} ({elec.
           broken.mean():.1%} of electricity)")

⏺ Edit Notebook(Homeworks/Homework 1/EDA.ipynb@0465847f)
  ⎿  Updated cell 16d27f8c:
         1 import lightgbm as lgb
         2
         3 FEATURES = [
         4     "meter", "primary_use", "square_feet", "year_built", "air_
           temperature", "dew_temperature",
         5     "sea_level_pressure", "wind_speed", "wind_direction", "hou
           r", "dayofweek",
         6 ]
         7 data["meter"] = data.meter.astype("category")
         8 data["hour"] = data.timestamp.dt.hour
         9 data["dayofweek"] = data.timestamp.dt.dayofweek
        10 data["log_reading"] = np.log1p(data.meter_reading)
        11
        12 is_val = data.timestamp >= "2016-10-01"
        13 train_part = data[~is_val].sample(3_000_000, random_state=0)
        14 val_part = data[is_val].sample(1_000_000, random_state=0)
        15
        16
        17 def fit_lgbm(rows):
        18     model = lgb.LGBMRegressor(
        19         n_estimators=500, learning_rate=0.1, num_leaves=63, ra
           ndom_state=0, verbose=-1
        20     )
        21     return model.fit(rows[FEATURES], rows.log_reading)
        22
        23
        24 def rmsle_log(y_log, pred_log):
        25     return np.sqrt(np.mean((np.clip(pred_log, 0, None) - y_log
           ) ** 2))
        26
        27
        28 model_a = fit_lgbm(train_part)
        29 model_b = fit_lgbm(train_part[~train_part.broken])
        30 val_part = val_part.assign(
        31     pred_a=model_a.predict(val_part[FEATURES]), pred_b=model_b
           .predict(val_part[FEATURES])
        32 )
        33
        34 is_elec = val_part.meter == 0
        35 subsets = {
        36     "all validation rows": np.ones(len(val_part), bool),
        37     "without broken electricity": ~val_part.broken,
        38     "electricity": is_elec,
        39     "electricity, not broken": is_elec & ~val_part.broken,
        40     "broken electricity only": val_part.broken,
        41 }
        42 display(
        43     pd.DataFrame(
        44         {
        45             name: {
        46                 "rows": mask.sum(),
        47                 "A: trained on all": rmsle_log(val_part.log_re
           ading[mask], val_part.pred_a[mask]),
        48                 "B: trained without broken": rmsle_log(val_par
           t.log_reading[mask], val_part.pred_b[mask]),
        49             }
        50             for name, mask in subsets.items()
        51         }
        52     ).T.round(3)
        53 )

  - Why it helps: most broken periods fall in January–September, the training
    months. Trained on them, the model learns that some buildings read zero and
    keeps predicting near-zero after those meters come back. Only 0.5% of
    validation rows are broken, so the gain on working meters far outweighs the
    small loss on broken ones.
  - Neither model can predict an outage (4.3 against 4.5 on the broken rows).
    The test period may have its own outages; nothing here can foresee them, and
    the validation share suggests they cost little.
  - The setups agree: model A's 1.395 matches the LightGBM in mlp_test.ipynb
    (1.392).

❯ Some other reccomendations on more careful preprocessing before training?
  What's about anomalies?

⏺ I'll check the data for a few anomaly types beyond zero runs before answering,
  so the recommendations are grounded in real numbers.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Beyond the broken electricity meters, the data has several other kinds of
  anomalies. I measured four on the merged training data:

  ┌───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │      Anomaly      │      How I detected it      │     Rows affected     │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Stuck meters: the │ identical reading for 48+   │ 306,275 (228K of them │
  │  same non-zero    │ consecutive hours           │  electricity)         │
  │ value repeated    │                             │                       │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Heating meters at │ steam or hot water at 0 for │                       │
  │  zero in winter   │  a week or more in          │ 25,017                │
  │                   │ January–February            │                       │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │                   │ reading more than 10× its   │ 32,750, spread across │
  │ Spikes            │ series' 24-hour rolling     │  all meters           │
  │                   │ median                      │                       │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ One extreme       │                             │ holds 78% of all      │
  │ series            │ building 1099's steam meter │ energy in train;      │
  │                   │                             │ peaks at 21.9M        │
  └───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  What each one means and what to do:

  1. Stuck meters are the biggest new finding. A real building's consumption
     never stays exactly constant, to the last decimal, for two days. These are
     frozen meters, or gaps that were filled with the last value before the data
     was published. They're worse than zeros in one way: RMSLE doesn't flag
     them as errors, so the model learns a flat, false pattern. Drop them from
     training, same as the broken zeros.
  2. Heating meters at zero in winter are broken, not off-season. A steam meter
     reading 0 for a week in February isn't a building that stopped heating. Use
     the same rule as electricity, but only in the heating season. For chilled
     water, apply it only in summer.
  3. Spikes: single-hour jumps of 10× are usually data errors, such as a meter
     reset or a catch-up reading after an outage. In log space they cost less,
     but they still pull the model. Either drop them, or clip each reading to a
     few times its series' rolling median.
  4. Building 1099 steam: its typical level (median 7,008) is high but within
     the normal range for steam meters (99th percentile 21,675). So its 78%
     share comes from a stretch of absurd values, up to 21.9M. Plot that series
     and drop or clip the bad stretch. With log1p it's only one series, but a
     very loud one.

  Other preprocessing worth adding:
  - Align weather time zones. Midnight at site 0 shows 25 °C, which suggests the
    weather timestamps are UTC while the meters are in local time. Check each
    site's peak-temperature hour (it should be around 14:00–15:00) and shift the
    weather by that site's offset before merging.
  - Flag imputed weather, and limit how far interpolation reaches. Site 5's
    pressure is entirely borrowed from other sites, and a gap of several days
    gets a straight line through it. Add a "filled" flag, or interpolate only
    gaps of a few hours and fill longer gaps from other sites.
  - Clean training data only, never the scoring. Anomaly rules and thresholds
    should come from the training period. Report validation RMSLE both on all
    rows and on "clean" rows, like §9 does, so it's clear what each cleaning
    step changes.
  - Test each cleaning step with the same A/B setup as §9, one rule at a time.
    That way you know which ones actually help, instead of stacking them
    blindly.

  If you want to pick one, I'd do stuck meters next. They affect about 1.5% of
  all rows, which is comparable in size to the broken zeros whose removal gave
  the biggest single improvement so far.

    14:00–16:00 at all 16 sites, which is when temperatures should peak.

    timestamps, so the meter timestamps need no shift.

  What's in §6.1:
  - A table per site with its location from the discussion, UTC offset, raw and
    shifted temperature peak hour, and raw electricity peak hour.
  - The fix: shift weather by each site's offset (local time = UTC + offset)
    before merging. Right now each reading at a North American site gets weather
    from 5–8 hours later.
  - Shared weather stations: sites 0 and 8 have identical weather, as do sites 7
    and 11. The discussion's "8: … same as Site 3" is a typo for site 0.
  - One caveat: I used standard-time offsets. If the meters follow daylight
    saving time, weather will be off by one hour in summer, which is minor next
    to the current 5–8 hours.

  The Summary (§10) now has a time-zones point.

❯ your reccomendation: how much should I really trust this discussion?

⏺ Trust it for what matters here, the UTC offsets, because our own data confirms
  them. Treat the exact city names as unverified; the model doesn't depend on
  them.

  Confirmed by our data (high trust):
  - Weather timestamps are UTC and meter timestamps are local. This doesn't
    depend on the discussion at all: raw temperature peaks at 19:00–23:00 at
    North American sites, and electricity peaks during the working day without
    any shift.
  - Each site's offset, to within about an hour. With the discussion's offsets,
    all 16 sites' temperature peaks land at 14:00–16:00 local time. The check is
    coarse, though. A peak hour can't cleanly separate −5 from −6, because the
    afternoon temperature curve is flat. So I'd trust the offsets to ±1 hour,
    not exactly.
  - Shared weather stations. Sites 0 & 8 and 7 & 11 have identical temperatures,
    which is a direct data fact.

  Plausible but unverified (medium trust):
  - Country per site (US, UK/Ireland, Canada). It fits the offsets: the three
    offset-0 sites peak at 14:00 raw, which is consistent with Europe. But
    offsets can't tell the UK from Ireland, or Montreal from New York. This
    matters only if you add holiday features, since the countries have different
    public holidays.

  Low trust, and not needed:
  - Exact cities. Several are marked "most likely" or "suspected" in the post
    itself, and the post has at least one visible slip (site 8 "same as Site
    3"). The model never uses city names directly.

  My recommendation:
  1. Use the offsets, since the data backs them. Keep the peak-hour check in the
     notebook, so a wrong offset would be visible.
  2. If you add holidays, use country only, and treat sites 7/11 (Canada vs US)
     and 12 (Ireland vs UK) as the least certain.
  3. Don't build anything on the exact cities.

  To pin the offsets down more tightly, you could try shifts of −1, 0 and +1
  hour around each site's offset and keep the one that gives the lowest
  validation RMSLE. That relies on your own data rather than the post, but it's
  an optional refinement: the big gain is fixing the 5–8 hour misalignment, and
  the offsets as given already do that.

  re-ran the notebook (about 2 minutes). Only two of the rules actually help a
  model: broken electricity and heating/cooling meters at zero when they're

  1.395 to 1.206, and almost all of that comes from those two.

    all rows and on clean rows only

  Anomaly: Broken electricity
  Rule: electricity at 0 for ≥ 1 week
  Rows flagged: 410K (3.4% of electricity)
  Validation RMSLE when dropped from training: 1.395 → 1.232
  Verdict: drop
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Anomaly: Off when needed
  Rule: steam/hot water at 0 for ≥ 1 week below 10 °C; chilled water the same
  above 20 °C
  Rows flagged: 69K

  Verdict: drop
  ────────────────────────────────────────

  Rows flagged: 306K

  Anomaly: Spike

  - Off when needed helps the meters it affects: steam improves from 1.638 to
    1.549 and chilled water from 1.588 to 1.552, both on clean validation rows.
    The example is building 1303's steam meter, at zero from mid-January to
    early June while the weekly mean temperature is mostly below 10 °C.
  - Stuck meters make no difference to this model. The rule does catch real
    frozen meters: building 144's electricity sits at one value from March to
    October. It may also flag legitimately flat readings, so it deserves a
    closer look before dropping those rows.
  - The spike rule is too blunt. Building 1021's hot water switches between 0
    and 10⁴–10⁵ all the time, and the rule flags the start of each "on" period.
    Removing spikes makes the score slightly worse.
  - Site 14 is in the top three sites for every rule. It's also the site with
    UTC meter timestamps, so its data deserves the closest look.

  These are single-seed runs, so differences under about 0.01 may be noise. The
  §10 summary now recommends dropping the two useful types.

❯ I think that spikes do not matter, while broken electricity is primary thing
  I should look at. What are your reccomendations

⏺ Agreed on both counts — the numbers back you up.

  Spikes: drop the idea, don't chase it. Removing flagged spikes from training
  made the score slightly worse (1.395 → 1.400), and the example plot showed
  why: the rule was mostly catching legitimate on/off cycling (building 1021's
  hot water swinging between 0 and 10⁴–10⁵), not real errors. It's not worth
  refining either — a smarter spike rule is more engineering for a problem that
  isn't costing you anything.

  Broken electricity is the right thing to focus on, since it's both the biggest
  single lever found so far (1.395 → 1.232 overall, 1.105 → 0.713 on clean
  electricity) and the most certain — a building never draws zero electricity
  for a week, so there's little ambiguity in the label.

  Where I'd take it next, in order:
  1. Just implement the drop. You already have the rule and the validated gain —
     add "electricity zero for ≥168h" as a training filter in
     data_aggregation.ipynb or wherever the MLP/LightGBM training data gets
     built, and carry it through to mlp_test.ipynb. This is the one change most
     likely to move the actual score.
  2. Check the threshold. 168 hours (1 week) was a round-number choice, not
     tuned. Worth a quick sensitivity check — try 24h, 72h, 168h, 336h as the
     cutoff and see where the validation gain plateaus. A shorter threshold
     catches more true outages but risks flagging brief legitimate dips.
  3. Site 0 dominates this anomaly (301K of the 410K flagged rows, 33% of its
     electricity). Given how concentrated it is, it's worth a quick look at
     whether site 0 needs special handling beyond just row-dropping — e.g., a
     days_since_meter_installed feature, or a flag for "this building had zero
     data for the first N months," in case some buildings only came online
     mid-year rather than being truly broken.
  4. Leave off-when-needed and stuck-meter as documented but not acted on for
     now — off-when-needed gave a smaller, real gain (worth doing eventually)
     but isn't where the leverage is; stuck-meter showed no effect and needs
     more investigation before it's trustworthy either way.

  If you want, I can implement the broken-electricity filter in
  data_aggregation.ipynb next, so it flows into all downstream training rather
  than living only as an EDA finding.

  "Working electricity meters" means validation electricity rows with a non-zero
  reading. It's the same set for every threshold, so it isolates how well each
  model predicts real consumption.

  - The score improves steadily as the threshold shortens, then levels off
    around 6 h. Dropping every zero and dropping runs of 6 h or more both give
    1.206.
  - Short zero runs hurt too. A building always draws some electricity, so even
    a few hours of zeros are gaps in the data feed, and they teach the model the
    same wrong lesson as long outages.
  - This single rule matches dropping all four anomaly types with the old 1-week
    rule: both reach 1.206.
  - It costs 3.2% of the training rows (96K of 3M).

  The §10 summary now recommends dropping every zero electricity reading. These
  are single-seed runs, but the gap between 1 h and 1 week (0.026) is consistent
  across all three validation columns, so it's unlikely to be noise.

  The next step is to apply this filter to the training data in
  data_aggregation.ipynb and mlp_test.ipynb.

         1 ## 2. Weather
         2
         3 - `cloud_coverage` and `precip_depth_1_hr` are dropped: often
           missing, and little or no correlation with the target
         4   (EDA §7).
         5 - Weather timestamps are UTC, meter timestamps are local (EDA
           §6.1). Weather is shifted by each site's UTC offset,
         6   except at site 14, whose meters are in UTC too.
         7 - Train and test weather are cleaned as one table, so the hour
           s around the train/test boundary use real weather
         8   after the shift instead of copies of the last value.
         9 - Some (site, hour) rows are absent, not just NaN, so each sit
           e is first reindexed onto a full hourly grid.
        10 - Gaps are filled by linear interpolation within each site.
        11 - A site with no data at all for a column (site 5 `sea_level_p
           ressure`) gets the mean of the other sites at the
        12   same hour. Pressure and wind come from weather systems that
           span large areas, so other sites are a reasonable
        13   proxy.
        14 - `wind_direction` is interpolated as sin/cos so that 350° → 1
           0° goes through 0°, not 180°.


  ⎿  Updated cell weather:
         1 # local time = UTC + offset (EDA §6.1); site 14 is 0 because i
           ts meters are in UTC too
         2 WEATHER_SHIFT_HOURS = {0: -5, 1: 0, 2: -7, 3: -5, 4: -8, 5: 0,
            6: -5, 7: -5,
         3                        8: -5, 9: -6, 10: -7, 11: -5, 12: 0, 13
           : -6, 14: 0, 15: -5}
         4
         5
         6 def clean_weather(w: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
         7     hours = pd.date_range(w.timestamp.min(), w.timestamp.max()
           , freq="h")
         8     grid = pd.MultiIndex.from_product(
         9         [w.site_id.unique(), hours], names=["site_id", "timest
           amp"]
        10     )
        11     w = w.set_index(["site_id", "timestamp"]).reindex(grid).re
           set_index()
        12
        13     rad = np.deg2rad(w.pop("wind_direction"))
        14     w["wd_sin"], w["wd_cos"] = np.sin(rad), np.cos(rad)
        15
        16     cols = w.columns.drop(["site_id", "timestamp"])
        17     w[cols] = w.groupby("site_id")[cols].transform(
        18         lambda s: s.interpolate(limit_direction="both")
        19     )
        20     w[cols] = w[cols].fillna(w.groupby("timestamp")[cols].tran
           sform("mean"))
        21
        22     w["wind_direction"] = np.rad2deg(np.arctan2(w.pop("wd_sin"
           ), w.pop("wd_cos"))) % 360
        23     return w.astype({c: "float32" for c in WEATHER_COLS})
        24
        25
        26 weather = pd.concat([weather_train, weather_test], ignore_inde
           x=True)
        27 weather["timestamp"] += pd.to_timedelta(weather.site_id.map(WE
           ATHER_SHIFT_HOURS), unit="h")
        28 weather_clean = clean_weather(weather)

⏺ Edit Notebook(Homeworks/Homework 1/data_aggregation.ipynb@84932af4)
  ⎿  Updated cell b70245d4:
         1 def flag_anomalies(df: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.Series:
         2     s = df.sort_values(["building_id", "meter", "timestamp"])
         3     series = s.building_id.astype(int) * 4 + s.meter.astype(in
           t)
         4     zero = s.meter_reading.eq(0)
         5     zero_run = (zero.ne(zero.shift()) | series.ne(series.shift
           ())).cumsum()  # consecutive zeros in one series
         6     run_hours = zero.groupby(zero_run).transform("size")
         7     run_temp = s.air_temperature.groupby(zero_run).transform("
           mean")
         8
         9     zero_electricity = zero & (s.meter == 0)
        10     off_when_needed = (zero & (run_hours >= 168)) & (
        11         (s.meter.isin([2, 3]) & (run_temp < 10)) | ((s.meter =
           = 1) & (run_temp > 20))
        12     )
        13     return (zero_electricity | off_when_needed).reindex(df.ind
           ex)
        14
        15
        16 train_merged["anomaly"] = flag_anomalies(train_merged)
        17 print(f"anomalies: {train_merged.anomaly.sum():,} rows ({train
           _merged.anomaly.mean():.1%} of train)")
        18 train_merged.groupby(train_merged.meter.map({0: "electricity",
            1: "chilledwater", 2: "steam", 3: "hotwater"})).anomaly.mean(
           ).mul(100).round(1).rename("% flagged")

  - The test set looks like the training data. Buildings, meters and the
    calendar can't be told apart. The weather is only separable in combination:
    exact values of temperature, pressure and wind together identify one
    specific hour of one year. That's a fingerprint, not a shift a model could
    learn from. No feature needs dropping.
  - Our validation split is shifted. The classifier separates our training and
    validation months almost perfectly, and all of that is weather, mainly
    temperature. So validation only tests autumn, while the test set covers
    whole years.

  How to tune the MLP with this

  1. Keep all current features, since none of them shift between training and
     test.
  2. Validate on every season. Split 2016 into four 3-month blocks, train on
     three, validate on the fourth, rotate, and choose settings by the average
     RMSLE over the four folds.
  3. Tune in this order: number of epochs (with early stopping), then learning
     rate, then regularization, then embedding size and width. Regularization
     matters because the best model reaches 0.80 on training but 1.11 on
     validation.
  4. Retrain on all of 2016 with the chosen settings before predicting the test
     set.
  5. Skip reweighting training rows by adversarial probability. That technique
     only helps when a feature has genuinely shifted, which isn't the case here.

  The fold setup in step 2 is the most useful next step, since every tuning
  decision depends on it. I can add it to mlp_test.ipynb.

❯ How should the weighting be performed?

⏺ Weighting makes training rows count more when they look like the test set, so
  the model focuses on the situations it will actually be scored on. It's done
  in five steps.

  1. Get a "test-likeness" probability for every training row. Train the
  train-vs-test classifier with cross-validation (e.g. 5 folds). Each training
  row then gets a prediction p = P(test | features) from a model that never saw
  that row. Out-of-fold predictions matter: a model scoring rows it was trained
  on is overconfident, and the weights become extreme.

  2. Turn the probability into a weight with the density ratio:

  w = p / (1 − p) × (n_train / n_test)

  The ratio p / (1 − p) estimates how much more common that kind of row is in
  test than in train. The n_train / n_test factor corrects for class balance in
  the classifier's training data. It drops out if you sampled equal numbers from
  each set, as the notebook does (500K each).

  3. Tame the weights. Clip them, e.g. to the 1st–99th percentile or a maximum
  of about 10, then divide by their mean so they average to 1. A few rows with
  huge weights would otherwise dominate training. Check the effective sample
  size, (Σw)² / Σw². If it's far below the row count, the weights are too
  extreme.

  4. Use them in the loss:
  - LightGBM: model.fit(X, y, sample_weight=w).
  - PyTorch MLP: weighted MSE, (w * (pred - y) ** 2).sum() / w.sum(), with the
    batch's weights sampled alongside the rows. Alternatively, sample rows with
    probability proportional to w and keep the plain loss.

  5. Weight the validation score the same way, so it estimates test performance
  rather than performance on your validation months.

  Why it wouldn't work here: the weights are only as meaningful as the
  classifier. Our train-vs-test classifier gets its AUC of 0.75 from weather
  fingerprints, i.e. exact value combinations that identify specific hours. So p
  would be high for 2016 hours whose weather happens to resemble some hour in
  2017–18, which is essentially random, and the weights would add noise to
  training.

  When it would work: when a feature has genuinely shifted, which shows up as a
  high AUC for that feature on its own. In that case, fit the classifier on only
  the shifted features, coarsened (e.g. temperature rounded to 5 °C bins), with
  a shallow model, so it can't memorize exact values.

  In our data, the one real shift is seasonal: our training months against our
  validation months, AUC 0.92. Validating on every season fixes that directly,
  and more simply than weighting.

❯ Where it should be performed? I.e. in which file?

⏺ If you did it, the work would be split between two files:

  - adversarial.ipynb (Homeworks/Homework%201/adversarial.ipynb) computes the
    weights. It already has the train-vs-test classifier. Add the out-of-fold
    predictions, the p / (1 − p) conversion, clipping and normalizing there.
    Then save one weight per training row, keyed by building_id, meter and
    timestamp, e.g. to data/processed/train_weights.parquet.
  - mlp_test.ipynb (Homeworks/Homework%201/mlp_test.ipynb) uses them. Join the
    weights onto the training rows after loading, then pass them to the loss:
    sample_weight for LightGBM, a weighted MSE for the MLP. The validation score
    gets the same weights.

  Two files shouldn't hold them:

  - data_aggregation.ipynb: it builds the dataset from raw files. Weights come
    out of a model, and they change whenever you change the classifier's
    features, so they don't belong in the base table next to the anomaly flag.
  - EDA.ipynb: it describes the data rather than producing inputs for training.

  For this data I still wouldn't build it, because the weights would be noise
  from the weather fingerprints. The four seasonal folds would go directly into
  mlp_test.ipynb and don't need any extra file.

❯ what to do with weather shift?

⏺ It depends on which of the two weather differences you mean. The seasonal one
  needs a change to validation, and the fingerprint one points to a feature
  change. Neither needs weather dropped.

  1. The seasonal shift in our split (training months vs validation months, AUC
  0.92)

  This comes from how the data was split, not from a flaw in the data. Training
  on January–September and validating on October–December means validation only
  ever sees autumn.
  - For tuning: use the four seasonal folds (train on three 3-month blocks,
    validate on the fourth). Every season then gets validated, and the average
    score reflects the whole year, like the test set.
  - For the final model: retrain on all of 2016. The test set covers full years,
    so the model should see every season. The shift only affects how you
    measure the model, not the model you submit.

  2. The fingerprint between training and test data (AUC 0.75)

  There's no real shift to correct, but it points to a risk: the model can
  memorize weather fingerprints too. With a building embedding plus exact hourly
  weather, it can learn "building X on the hour with this exact temperature and
  pressure" instead of "building X in cold weather". That fits the gap in the
  best model: 0.80 RMSLE on training, 1.11 on validation.

  What I'd do about it:
  - Add smoothed weather, such as rolling 24-hour and 7-day mean temperature per
    site. Smoothed values are harder to memorize, and they capture the fact
    that buildings heat and cool slowly, so recent days' weather drives today's
    load. The top solution you shared used smoothed temperature features for
    this reason.
  - Regularize the MLP with dropout and weight decay, tuned on the seasonal
    folds.
  - Check the result: after adding smoothed features, if validation improves
    while training gets slightly worse, the model was memorizing, and the change
    helped.

  If you pick one, I'd do the seasonal folds first. Without them you can't
  reliably tell whether the smoothed features or the regularization actually
  help.

⏺ Written for: you, as a reusable course reference, in the style of the other
  guides in Development Guide/.

  I wrote Regularization_guide.md (Development%20Guide/Regularization_guide.md)
  and linked it from §4.5 of DL_Development_Pipeline.md
  (Development%20Guide/DL_Development_Pipeline.md), the same way the EDA and
  validation guides are linked. It builds on the Lecture 4 regularization
  notebook and adds a practical order of work.

  General regularization:
  - Diagnose first: a small table maps training vs validation curve patterns to
    what to do. Regularization only helps when the model is overfitting, not
    when it's underfitting.
  - The toolbox, cheapest first: label cleaning, early stopping, weight decay
    (with AdamW), dropout, smaller capacity, regularizing embeddings, input
    noise and ensembling. Each comes with typical settings, plus LightGBM's
    equivalent knobs.
  - How to tune: fix validation first, change one knob at a time, use 2–3 seeds,
    and watch both curves.

  Seasonal shift:
  - Validation that exposes it: blocks of consecutive months, choosing settings
    by the mean score across folds and preferring those with a small spread.
  - Features that behave the same in every season: heating and cooling
    degree-hours, smoothed and lagged weather, no date-like features the model
    can only memorize, and per-building normalization of the target.
  - Model-side: stronger regularization on identity embeddings, one output per
    meter type, monotone constraints for trees, and averaging the fold models.
  - What not to do: tune on a single season, or reweight by adversarial
    probability.

  Worked example: it applies the guide to this project with our real numbers.
  - The diagnosis: 0.80 training vs 1.11 validation RMSLE.
  - The biggest win so far was data cleaning: 1.365 → 1.108.
  - The shift: AUC 0.92 between our training and validation months.
  - A six-step plan: seasonal folds, then early stopping, then weight decay,
    then dropout, then weather features, then retraining on the full year.

  The linter flags spacing issues in the pipeline guide, but those are in the
  file's existing style; my one added line follows it.

❯ How now weight initialization works?

⏺ The MLPs in mlp_test.ipynb don't set initialization explicitly, so they use
  PyTorch's defaults. torch.manual_seed(0) before each model makes every model
  start from the same random draw.

  ┌─────────────────┬────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │      Layer      │      Default       │         Resulting scale          │
  │                 │   initialization   │                                  │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ nn.Linear       │ uniform on         │ std = 1/√(3·fan_in); for 64      │
  │ weights         │ ±1/√fan_in         │ inputs, ≈ 0.072                  │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ nn.Linear bias  │ uniform on         │ small, non-zero                  │
  │                 │ ±1/√fan_in         │                                  │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ nn.BatchNorm1d  │ scale 1, shift 0   │ starts as plain normalization    │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ nn.Embedding    │ normal(0, 1)       │ std 1, the same scale as the     │
  │                 │                    │ standardized inputs              │
  └─────────────────┴────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  Here fan_in is the number of inputs to the layer.

  How this compares with the lecture: for ReLU networks, Lecture 4 recommends He
  (Kaiming) initialization, with std √(2/fan_in). PyTorch's default is about
  2.4× smaller. Without normalization, each ReLU layer then shrinks the signal,
  so in deep networks the activations and gradients fade layer by layer.

  Why it doesn't matter much here:
  - The models with BatchNorm (mlp_bn, mlp_4out and both building-identity
    models) re-normalize every hidden layer, so the initial weight scale barely
    affects the signal.
  - The plain mlp has only 2 hidden layers, so a smaller-than-ideal scale
    shrinks the signal twice, not dozens of times. Adam's per-parameter step
    sizes also make up for a poor starting scale quickly.
  - The embedding's std of 1 matches the standardized features, so neither
    dominates the first layer at the start.

  If you want it explicit, for the assignment's weight-initialization
  requirement (pipeline §4.2) or for deeper networks, add He initialization:

  def init_he(module):
      if isinstance(module, nn.Linear):
          nn.init.kaiming_normal_(module.weight, nonlinearity="relu")
          nn.init.zeros_(module.bias)

  model.apply(init_he)

  For the final output layer, which has no ReLU after it, a small scale is
  better; nn.init.xavier_normal_ works. Running the plain mlp with default vs He
  initialization under the same seed would show the effect directly. I'd expect
  a small difference at this depth, mostly in the first epoch or two.

⏺ I'll add He-initialized copies of the two building-identity models. Everything
  else stays identical: same seed, same data, same training. The comparison
  with the default-initialized versions then isolates the initialization.

⏺ I added He initialization to both building-identity models in mlp_test.ipynb
  (Homeworks/Homework%201/mlp_test.ipynb) and ran it. It makes no real
  difference: the scores are the same or slightly worse than with PyTorch's
  default initialization.

  Both versions use the same seed, data and training. The He versions use He
  initialization for the hidden layers, Xavier for the output layer and zero
  biases; the embedding keeps its default.

  ┌───────────────┬──────────────┬─────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │     Model     │ Default init │ He init │ Epoch 1, default → He │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ mlp_4out_emb  │ 1.108        │ 1.108   │ 1.149 → 1.166         │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ mlp_4out_mean │ 1.118        │ 1.122   │ 1.136 → 1.154         │
  └───────────────┴──────────────┴─────────┴───────────────────────┘

  - He initialization starts slower and catches up. Epoch 1 is about 0.02 worse
    for both models, and by epochs 4–5 the gap is gone.
  - Why: both models use BatchNorm, which re-normalizes every hidden layer, so
    the starting weight scale barely matters in a 2-layer network. He
    initialization pays off in deeper networks without normalization.
  - The differences are within noise. Training on the Mac's GPU (MPS) isn't
    fully deterministic: the default embedding model scored 1.1075 in the
    previous run and 1.1084 in this one, with the same code and seed.

  The notebook has a short section explaining the two initializations, and the
  written comparison now includes this finding. For the assignment's
  initialization requirement, the He code is in place and documented. For this
  model's score, regularization and the seasonal folds are where the gains are.

      11  | Option | Use when | Cost |
      12 -|---|---|---|                                                  
      12 +| --- | --- | --- |                                            
      13  | **Plain PyTorch loop** | one quick experiment, data already i
          n memory, full control needed | you write checkpointing, loggin
          g, early stopping yourself |
      14  | **PyTorch Lightning** | most projects: several models, folds,
           callbacks, logging, multi-GPU later | learn its structure (`Li
          ghtningModule`, `Trainer`) |
      15  | **Hugging Face `Trainer`** | models and datasets from the `tr
          ansformers` / `datasets` ecosystem | the model must return a di
          ct with `loss`; less flexible outside NLP/vision |
     ...
      19  ## 2. Data Flow: Dataset → DataCollator → DataLoader
      20  
      21  | Part | Job | Keep out of it |
      22 -|---|---|---|                                                  
      22 +| --- | --- | --- |                                            
      23  | **Dataset** | read raw data, return **one** sample by index |
           batching, GPU work |
      24  | **DataCollator** | turn a list of samples into a batch: to te
          nsors, pad/normalize, return a dict (`{"pixel_values": ..., "la
          bels": ...}`) | `.to(device)` — collators run inside CPU worker
           processes |
      25  | **DataLoader** | batching, shuffling, parallel workers (`num_
          workers`), `collate_fn=collator` | model logic |
      26  
      27  Practical rules from the lecture:
      28 +                                                               
      29  - Move batches to the GPU in the **main process**: `x.to(device
          , non_blocking=True)`, paired with `pin_memory=True` in the Dat
          aLoader (CUDA only).
      30  - `num_workers = os.cpu_count() - 1` is a reasonable start for 
          data read from disk.
      31  - Put transforms in the collator when different loaders need di
          fferent policies (e.g. augmentation for training only).
     ...
      40  - **`Trainer`**: runs the loop. Everything else is configuratio
          n: `max_epochs`, `accelerator="auto"`, `logger=...`, `callbacks
          =[...]`.
      41  
      42  Callbacks worth using from day one:
      43 +                                                               
      44  - `ModelCheckpoint(monitor="val_loss", mode="min", save_top_k=1
          )` — keeps the best epoch, not the last.
      45  - `EarlyStopping(monitor="val_loss", patience=3)` — stops when 
          validation stops improving.
      46  - `LearningRateMonitor(logging_interval="step")` — logs the LR 
          so schedules can be checked.

⏺ Written for: you, as a reusable course reference alongside the other guides in
  Development Guide/.

  I wrote Frameworks_guide.md (Development%20Guide/Frameworks_guide.md), based
  on the Lecture 5 notebook (Frameworks.ipynb), and linked it from §5 of
  DL_Development_Pipeline.md (Development%20Guide/DL_Development_Pipeline.md).

  What it covers:
  - Choosing a training loop: plain PyTorch vs PyTorch Lightning vs Hugging Face
    Trainer, with when each fits and what it costs.
  - Data flow: what the Dataset, DataCollator and DataLoader should each do, and
    keep out of. It includes the lecture's rules (no GPU work in worker
    processes; pin_memory with non_blocking), plus one exception the lecture
    doesn't cover: tabular data that fits in memory is faster indexed directly
    than loaded row by row.
  - Lightning: the LightningModule / LightningDataModule / Trainer split, the
    callbacks worth using from the start (best-epoch checkpoint, early stopping,
    learning-rate logging), and the torchmetrics update → compute → reset
    pattern.
  - Hugging Face Trainer: the model contract (return a dict with loss),
    TrainingArguments and compute_metrics.
  - Logging: the lecture's comparison of W&B, TensorBoard, MLflow and CSV, and
    what to log for every run, including failed ones.
  - Training tricks: one table covering gradient clipping, gradient
    accumulation, SWA, DDP, FSDP and mixed precision, each with its one-line
    Lightning setting. It uses Lightning 2.x's precision names ("16-mixed",
    "bf16-mixed").
  - Reproducibility: seeding and deterministic settings, plus measuring
    run-to-run noise when the hardware isn't deterministic.
  - Worked example on this project: why mlp_test.ipynb keeps its data on the GPU
    and indexes it directly, what Lightning would add once the seasonal folds
    arrive, the Mac GPU's run-to-run noise, the PyTorch + LightGBM crash and its
    fix, and why none of the training tricks are needed at this model size.

  I checked your environment: Lightning 2.6.5 is installed, and both import
  paths (lightning.pytorch and the lecture's pytorch_lightning) work. I also
  fixed the markdown formatting the linter flagged in the new guide.
