⏺ I reimplemented mlp_test.ipynb in mlp_frameworks.ipynb
  (Homeworks/Homework%201/mlp_frameworks.ipynb) using PyTorch Lightning and
  MLflow. The results match: the model ranking is identical, and every MLP is
  within 0.007 RMSLE of mlp_test, which is inside the run-to-run noise we
  measured there. The notebook runs end-to-end in about 2 minutes.

  ┌───────────────┬──────────┬────────────────┐
  │     Model     │ mlp_test │ mlp_frameworks │
  ├───────────────┼──────────┼────────────────┤
  │ mlp_4out_emb  │ 1.108    │ 1.112          │
  ├───────────────┼──────────┼────────────────┤
  │ mlp_4out_mean │ 1.118    │ 1.122          │
  ├───────────────┼──────────┼────────────────┤
  │ lgbm          │ 1.166    │ 1.166          │
  ├───────────────┼──────────┼────────────────┤
  │ mlp_4out      │ 1.416    │ 1.409          │
  ├───────────────┼──────────┼────────────────┤
  │ mlp_bn        │ 1.424    │ 1.421          │
  ├───────────────┼──────────┼────────────────┤
  │ mlp           │ 1.442    │ 1.443          │
  └───────────────┴──────────┴────────────────┘

  The small differences come from a different shuffle order and from the Mac GPU
  not being fully deterministic. LightGBM and the baseline don't use PyTorch,
  so they match exactly.

  How it's built:
  - Data loading: the Dataset returns a whole batch per call (__getitems__), so
    the DataLoader shuffles and batches 3M rows without fetching them one at a
    time.
  - Networks: all of them take the same inputs, so one LightningModule trains
    any of them. Validation RMSLE is computed over the full epoch with
    torchmetrics.
  - Training: a fresh Trainer and MLflow logger per model. Each run logs its
    settings, per-epoch training and validation RMSLE, and per-meter scores. The
    baseline and LightGBM are logged with the plain MLflow API.
  - Results: the comparison table and the learning curves are read back from
    MLflow, filtered to the current run of the notebook.

  I left out the He-initialization variants, which made no difference, and the
  test-set submission, which mlp_test.ipynb still produces.

  Setup changes you should know about:
  - MLflow is now a dependency (MLflow 3.16). I added it with poetry add mlflow,
    which changed pyproject.toml and poetry.lock.
  - LightGBM is imported only in its own cell. Importing it before PyTorch
    crashed the kernel on macOS, and a comment in the notebook explains why.
  - mlflow.db is created next to the notebook and isn't git-ignored. It holds
    the runs from the three times I executed the notebook. You might want to add
    mlflow.db and mlruns/ to .gitignore.

  To browse the runs, start the MLflow UI from the Homework 1 folder and open
  http://127.0.0.1:5000:

  poetry run mlflow ui --backend-store-uri sqlite:///mlflow.db

❯ How ML flow runs locally? Is not it paid thing?

⏺ MLflow is free and open source — there's no paid tier for what we're using.
  It's easy to confuse with Weights & Biases, which does have a paid hosted
  product; MLflow is different.

  How it runs here, concretely:
  - mlflow.set_tracking_uri("sqlite:///mlflow.db") tells MLflow to store
    everything in a local SQLite file, mlflow.db, sitting next to the notebook.
    No server, no account, no network call.
  - Every mlflow.start_run() or Lightning's MLFlowLogger just writes rows into
    that SQLite file: parameters, metrics, per-epoch history.
  - poetry run mlflow ui --backend-store-uri sqlite:///mlflow.db starts a small
    local web server (Flask) on 127.0.0.1:5000 that reads that same file and
    renders it as a dashboard in your browser. It's not talking to any external
    service — it's just a local viewer for the local file.

  Where the confusion might come from: Databricks (a company) offers a hosted,
  managed MLflow service as part of their paid platform, with extra enterprise
  features (access control, model registry hosting, etc.). But the MLflow
  library and server you get from pip install mlflow / poetry add mlflow is
  Apache-2.0 licensed and fully usable standalone, which is exactly what we did.

  Storage backends, for context: SQLite (what we used) is the simplest option
  for one person on one machine. MLflow also supports Postgres/MySQL for a
  shared team backend, and artifact storage on S3/GCS/local disk — all still
  free, just more infrastructure to run yourself if you want multiple people
  sharing one tracking server.

  So nothing here required payment or an account — it's entirely local and free.

⏺ I merged both MLP notebooks into mlp.ipynb (Homeworks/Homework%201/mlp.ipynb),
  rewritten in the top_post.md style. It runs end-to-end in under 3 minutes,
  and the scores match the previous runs to within about 0.004.

  How it reads now:
  - Short sections with plain prose: Data → Features → Models → Training →
    Results → Findings → What didn't work → Test predictions.
  - Frameworks at the bottom: the header links to a Frameworks section that
    explains PyTorch Lightning and MLflow, including that MLflow is free and
    runs entirely locally.
  - Baseline removed.

  Simpler code:
  - Two network classes instead of four: MLP (with optional BatchNorm) and
    MeterMLP (4 outputs, with an optional building embedding).
  - One train() loop covers all five MLPs.
  - LightGBM is one cell, with its crash workaround commented.
  - Results are read back from MLflow: one table, one plot of the learning
    curves.

  Findings, in short:
  - Knowing the building matters most: about 1.41 → 1.11, and both
    building-aware models beat LightGBM (1.17).
  - Clean data came before any architecture choice: it improved every model by
    0.11–0.26.
  - Architecture tweaks help a little: BatchNorm and one output per meter gain
    0.01–0.02 each.
  - Hot water stays hardest.
  - The building-aware models overfit: about 0.8–0.9 on training vs 1.11 on
    validation.
  - What didn't work: He initialization.

⏺ I'll call the notebook regularization_test.ipynb, correcting the spelling. It
  applies the Regularization guide to mlp_4out_emb and is measured the way the
  guide recommends:
  - Validation: four seasonal folds, one per 3-month block. Train on the other
    nine months, validate on the block, and report the mean and spread across
    folds.
  - Setups compared: no regularization, weight decay, dropout, a smaller
    building embedding, and all three combined.
  - Early stopping: every setup trains for 10 epochs, and the per-epoch
    validation curve logged to MLflow shows what stopping at the best epoch
    would give.

⏺ I created regularization_test.ipynb
  (Homeworks/Homework%201/regularization_test.ipynb), spelled "regularization"
  rather than "regulatization". It applies the Regularization guide to
  mlp_4out_emb, and the result is useful even though it's negative: none of the
  regularization setups improved validation, because this model isn't
  overfitting in the usual sense.

  How it's measured: four seasonal folds. Each fold trains on nine months and
  validates on the other three, so every season is tested once. Each run trains
  for 10 epochs and is logged to MLflow; all 24 take about 8 minutes.

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────┬───────────────┬─────────────┐
  │        Setup         │ Mean validation  │ Spread across │  Training   │
  │                      │      RMSLE       │     folds     │    RMSLE    │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ none                 │ 1.200            │ 0.164         │ 0.827       │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ weight decay 0.01    │ 1.200            │ 0.164         │ 0.827       │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ weight decay 0.1     │ 1.202            │ 0.166         │ 0.828       │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ dropout 0.2          │ 1.208            │ 0.159         │ 0.972       │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ embedding of 4       │ 1.209            │ 0.161         │ 0.848       │
  │ instead of 16        │                  │               │             │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ all three combined   │ 1.231            │ 0.148         │ 1.005       │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────┴───────────────┴─────────────┘

  - Early stopping has nothing to stop. Validation is still improving at epoch
    9–10 in every setup.
  - The train–validation gap is mostly a difference between seasons, not
    memorization. Validation ranges from 1.05 (Jul–Sep) to 1.42 (Jan–Mar)
    depending on which months are held out. That's about ten times more than any
    setup changes it.
  - Weight decay does nothing, even at 0.1. Most weights sit right before a
    BatchNorm layer, which rescales their output anyway.
  - Dropout and the smaller embedding shrink the gap by raising training error,
    not by lowering validation error.
  - The folds caught a false win. On October–December alone, dropout looks
    better (1.111 → 1.102), but it loses on the other three seasons.

  What to try next: features that behave the same in every season, such as
  heating and cooling degree-hours and smoothed weather, and longer training,
  since validation is still improving at epoch 10. These are single-seed runs,
  so differences under about 0.01 may be noise.

  I also added this result to the worked example in Regularization_guide.md. It
  had described what success would look like, and now records what actually
  happened.

❯ Explain what do you mean by "lesson: diagnose 1 first"

⏺ It refers to §1 of the Regularization guide, "Diagnose Before You Regularize".
  The lesson is to look at the learning curves before choosing a fix, because
  regularization only solves one specific problem.

  What §1 says: plot training and validation loss per epoch, then read the
  pattern. Regularization is the fix for one pattern only: training loss keeps
  falling while validation stops improving or gets worse. That means the model
  is memorizing the training data.

  What our curves showed: both training and validation were still falling at
  epoch 10, in every setup. That's not the overfitting pattern, so there was
  nothing for regularization to fix.

  Why the gap was still large (0.83 vs 1.20): a gap alone doesn't mean
  overfitting. Here validation is a different season from training, and seasons
  differ a lot, so validation error ranged from 1.05 to 1.42 depending on which
  months were held out. A large gap from a harder, different validation period
  calls for a different fix: features that behave the same in every season (§4
  of the guide), not penalties.

  The lesson in one sentence: I read "big gap" as overfitting and tried the
  overfitting remedies, but the curves already showed it wasn't overfitting.
  Checking the pattern first, as §1 says, would have predicted the result before
  spending 24 training runs.

  "Diagnose with §1 first" is too compressed. Want me to reword that line in the
  guide as: "check the learning curves (§1) before choosing a fix: here both
  curves were still falling, so the gap came from seasonal differences, not
  memorization"?

⏺ I created optimizers_test.ipynb (Homeworks/Homework%201/optimizers_test.ipynb)
  and ran it. It trains mlp_4out_mean and mlp_4out_emb with SGD, SGD with
  momentum, Nesterov and Adam, for 10 epochs each. Adam is the best optimizer
  overall, but only because of the embedding model; for the series-mean model,
  the optimizer barely matters. The 8 runs take about 3.5 minutes, and all are
  logged to MLflow.

  Validation RMSLE after 10 epochs:

  ┌───────────┬───────────────┬──────────────┐
  │ Optimizer │ mlp_4out_mean │ mlp_4out_emb │
  ├───────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ SGD       │ 1.111         │ 1.134        │
  ├───────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Momentum  │ 1.114         │ 1.117        │
  ├───────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Nesterov  │ 1.112         │ 1.121        │
  ├───────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Adam      │ 1.113         │ 1.102        │
  └───────────┴───────────────┴──────────────┘

  Plots, as you asked: first one panel per model, comparing all four optimizers'
  validation curves. Then both models together with Adam, showing validation
  (solid) and training (dashed).

  What it shows:
  - For mlp_4out_mean, the optimizer doesn't matter: all four finish within
    0.003, less than the wobble between epochs. The series-mean input already
    carries most of the signal.
  - For mlp_4out_emb, Adam leads at every epoch. The likely reason: each
    building's embedding only gets gradient from that building's rows. Adam
    scales each parameter's step to its own gradient size, while SGD uses one
    learning rate for everything.
  - Momentum and Nesterov steady plain SGD, whose validation score jumps around,
    up to 1.21 at epoch 2. The two end up close to each other.
  - With Adam, validation improves only slightly after epoch 2 (about 0.02),
    while training error keeps falling. That's the same seasonal gap found in
    regularization_test.ipynb.

  Each optimizer used one common learning rate for its type (SGD 0.1, momentum
  and Nesterov 0.01, Adam 0.001), not a tuned one, on a single split and seed. A
  tuned SGD might close part of the gap on the embedding model.

❯ Is ther eexplanation why they are so unstable?

⏺ Yes. Several things combine here, most likely in this order of importance:

  1. The learning rate never decreases. Each step uses one mini-batch of 4,096
  rows, so its gradient is a noisy estimate of the true one. With a constant
  learning rate, the weights never settle into a minimum; they keep bouncing
  around it, and the size of that bounce grows with the step size. Plain SGD at
  learning rate 0.1 takes the largest raw steps, so it bounces most. Adam
  bounces too (±0.004), just less. This is exactly why the lecture recommends
  learning-rate schedules: a decaying rate, such as cosine annealing, shrinks
  the bouncing as training ends.

  2. Validation is a different season, which magnifies small weight changes. The
  training curves are smooth; only validation wobbles. Near the end of
  training, many slightly different weight settings fit January–September almost
  equally well, but they extrapolate to October–December differently. So a
  small move that barely changes training loss can move validation RMSLE by
  0.02.

  3. BatchNorm's running statistics lag behind the weights. At validation time,
  BatchNorm uses averages of the batch statistics collected during training,
  updated gradually as the weights change. When the weights take big steps, as
  with SGD at a high learning rate, those averages are always slightly out of
  date for the current weights. That adds extra noise to validation scores.

  4. Embedding rows get noisy updates. Each building's embedding is updated only
  by the few rows of that building in each batch. With SGD's single global
  learning rate, those updates are both small and noisy. Adam normalizes them
  per parameter, which fits its clear lead on the embedding model.

  How to check: run the embedding model with SGD twice, with a constant learning
  rate and with cosine decay to near zero, and compare the epoch-to-epoch
  spread over the last 5 epochs. If the decayed version is much steadier,
  explanation 1 dominates. Stochastic Weight Averaging (averaging the weights
  over the last epochs, from Lecture 5) should steady it too. Want me to add
  that test to optimizers_test.ipynb?
